Graphical functions in even dimensions
Michael Borinsky and Oliver Schnetz

Abstract
. Graphical functions are special position space Feynman integrals, which can be used to calculate Feynman periods and one- or two-scale processes at high loop orders. With graphical functions, renormalization constants have been calculated to loop orders seven and eight in four-dimensional ϕ 4 \phi^{4} theory and to order five in six-dimensional ϕ 3 \phi^{3} theory. In this article we present the theory of graphical functions in even dimensions ≥ 4 \geq 4 with detailed reviews of known properties and full proofs whenever possible.



Nikhef-2021-007

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Motivation

Graphical functions are massless position space Feynman integrals in quantum field theory (QFT) which depend on three vectors
z_{0}
,
z_{1}
,
z_{2}
in
D
-dimensional Euclidean space.
They are a powerful tool to perform calculations in perturbative QFT. Graphical functions were first defined in
[
33
]
but also play a prominent role in four-dimensional conformal
QFTs where they were independently introduced (see e.g.
[
19
]
).

The theory of graphical functions has successfully been applied to four-dimensional
\phi^{4}
theory (see e.g.
[
15
,
33
,
35
]
). Calculations of Feynman periods
in
\phi^{4}
theory led to the discovery of the coaction conjectures in
[
30
]
and the coaction principle (see e.g.
[
12
,
13
,
36
]
).

In this article, we provide an extension of graphical functions to even dimensions
\geq 4
. This extension made six dimensional
\phi^{3}
theory accessible to higher loop orders
(the loop order is the number of independent cycles in the underlying Feynman graph). Without the theory of graphical functions
\phi^{3}
is a notoriously hard subject.
The authors calculated all primitive periods in
\phi^{3}
theory up to loop order six
[
6
,
37
]
.
At loop order seven, 561 of the 607 primitive Feynman periods were computed. At loop orders eight and nine many sporadic results exist
[
6
,
37
]
.
These period calculations were the basis for a recent computation of the renormalization constants in
\phi^{3}
theory up to loop order
five with applications to percolation theory
[
5
]
(see
[
26
]
for the independent classical calculation).
Recently, higher dimensional graphical functions also found applications in the context of graph complexes
[
14
]
.

Graphical functions also exist in odd dimensions
\geq 3
. Their theory differs from the even dimensional case and is much less explored.
The case of two dimensions is singular and needs special treatment
[
33
]
.
For the physical QFTs in the standard model, graphical functions in even dimensions
\geq 4
seem to suffice.

Graphical functions also led to the seven loop results for the renormalization constants in
\phi^{4}
theory
[
35
,
39
]
.
Recently, the eight loop calculation of the gamma function was finalized
[
37
]
.
Renormalization demands an extension to non-integer dimensions as a regularization mechanism, see e.g.
[
21
,
23
]
.
In the context of graphical functions this dimensional regularization is very convenient for calculations.
A precise definition of dimensional regularization, however, requires the use of the parametric representation which can be unwieldy for mathematical proofs.
Therefore, the theory of dimensionally regularized graphical functions mostly relies on a series of (well tested) conjectures.

In this article, we focus on graphical functions in even integer dimension
D\geq 4
, where most results can be proved:

(1)

D=2\lambda+2,\quad\lambda\in\{1,2,3,\ldots\}.

We also restrict ourselves to scalar graphical functions. Graphical functions with positive spin can be expressed as tuples of scalar graphical functions.
By a dimension shift mechanism, calculations of graphical functions representing Feynman integrals of particles with positive spin demand the handling of scalar graphical
functions in higher even dimensions. Hence, this article also paves the ground for future calculations in QFTs with spin (such as Yang-Mills theories).

1.2.
The graphical function method

Let
G
be a graph with edge set
\mathcal{E}_{G}
and vertex set
\mathcal{V}_{G}
which splits into internal vertices
{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}
and external vertices
{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{ext}}}
.
We assume that
G
has exactly three external vertices and label the vertices by vectors in
D
-dimensional Euclidean space,
{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{ext}}}=\{z_{0},z_{1},z_{2}\}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
.
The internal labels are
x_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
,
i=1,2,\ldots,{|{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}|}
.

Every edge
e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}
has a weight
\nu_{e}\in{\mathbb{R}}
. We often assume
\lambda\nu_{e}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Although Feynman graphs in scalar QFTs only have unit edge-weights, it is convenient to allow general weights.
Weights of multiple edges are additive: if
e,f\in\mathcal{E}_{G}
with weights
\nu_{e},\nu_{f}
have equal endpoints, the pair
e,f
can be replaced by a single edge with the same endpoints
and weight
\nu_{e}+\nu_{f}
. Zero weight edges can be eliminated from the graph
G
. We do not allow
G
to have self-loops.

To every edge
e=uv\in\mathcal{E}_{G}
we associate a quadric which is the square of the distance between the labels of its endpoints
u,v\in\mathcal{V}_{G}
,

(2)

Q_{e}(u,v)=\|u-v\|^{2}=(u^{1}-v^{1})^{2}+\ldots+(u^{D}-v^{D})^{2}.

With this data we write the position space three-point function
A_{G}
as a positive integral
[
23
]
,

(3)

A_{G}(z_{0},z_{1},z_{2})=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{|{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}|}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{D}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{D}x_{i}}{\pi^{D/2}}\right)\frac{1}{\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}Q_{e}^{\lambda\nu_{e}}}.

In this article we only consider graphs
G
for which the above integral converges. This significantly restricts the set of admissible graphs, see Proposition
11
.

x^{1}
x^{2}
x^{D}
z_{0}
z_{1}
z_{2}
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Re}z
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Im}z
{\mathbb{C}}
0
1
z
1
|z|
|z-1|

Figure 1.
The vectors
z_{0},z_{1},z_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
span a plane that is identified with the complex plane
{\mathbb{C}}
by requiring
z_{0},z_{1}
to coincide with
0,1\in{\mathbb{C}}
.
The position of the vector
z_{2}
inside the plane determines the value
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
(up to conjugation). Comparing ratios of squared side lengths of the triangles
z_{0},z_{1},z_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}

and
0,1,z\in{\mathbb{C}}
gives the relations in (
4
).

The value of
A_{G}(z_{0},z_{1},z_{2})
is invariant under simultaneous translations and rotations of the vectors
z_{0},z_{1},z_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
and it is homogeneous under scaling.
An efficient parametrization is obtained by the identification of the affine plane spanned by
z_{0},z_{1},z_{2}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
with
{\mathbb{C}}
.
We assume that
z_{0}
and
z_{1}
coincide with 0 and 1 in
{\mathbb{C}}
(setting the scale in
{\mathbb{C}}
, see Figure
1
).
The third vector
z_{2}
is associated with a variable
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
in one of the two possible ways (the ambiguity under complex conjugation leads to the symmetry (G1) in Theorem
5
).
The graphical function
f_{G}(z)
is the Feynman integral
A_{G}
evaluated on the complex plane
{\mathbb{C}}
.

We obtain the following relations between
z_{0},z_{1},z_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
and
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
:

(4)

z{\overline{z}}=\frac{\|z_{2}-z_{0}\|^{2}}{\|z_{1}-z_{0}\|^{2}},\quad(z-1)({\overline{z}}-1)=\frac{\|z_{2}-z_{1}\|^{2}}{\|z_{1}-z_{0}\|^{2}},

where
{\overline{z}}
is the complex conjugate of
z
. With these relations we get

(5)

f_{G}(z)=\|z_{1}-z_{0}\|^{(D-2)\sum_{e}\nu_{e}-D{|{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}|}}A_{G}(z_{0},z_{1},z_{2}).

By power-counting the pre-factor on the right hand side compensates for the scaling behavior of the Feynman integral
A_{G}
.

1.3.
Statement of results

In Section
2
we define graphical functions and summarize their fundamental properties in Theorem
5
: Graphical functions are single-valued real-analytic functions
on
{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\{0,1\}
(see
[
21
]
) which admit series expansions at their singular points
0,1
and
\infty
of log-Laurent type (
18
) and (
20
).
The expansion at infinity lifts graphical functions to objects on the (punctured) Riemann sphere
{\mathbb{C}}\cup\{\infty\}
.

Theorem
5
, in spite of its technical nature, is a cornerstone of the theory of graphical functions. The proof is based on the expansion (
110
) of graphical functions
into Gegenbauer polynomials (see
[
17
]
) using radial and angular graphical functions (Sections
16
–
20
). A full proof of (
110
) is only provided in the
classical case of four dimensions and unit edge-weights, see Theorems
80
and
84
. The general case is well tested but formally relies on the validity of
interchanging the Gegenbauer expansion with the position space integrals, see Conjecture
86
.

Integration of
z
over the complex plane
\int_{\mathbb{C}}\mathrm{d}^{2}z
converts graphical functions into numbers: Feynman periods
[
33
,
35
]
.
The calculation of Feynman periods is a classical subject in QFT (see e.g.
[
7
,
34
]
).
Feynman periods are essential ingredients in the calculation of renormalization constants which, in turn, allow one to calculate critical exponents for statistical models
[
44
]
.
In Section
3
we describe how Feynman periods can be obtained from graphical functions.

Note that graphical functions were originally devised in
[
33
]
for the purpose of computing Feynman periods.
Lists of Feynman periods in four-dimensional
\phi^{4}
theory up to loop order eight can be found in
[
34
,
35
]
. A list of periods in six-dimensional
\phi^{3}
theory up to loop
order nine is in
[
37
]
which also extends the
\phi^{4}
data to loop order eleven.

In Sections
5
to
15
we report on known identities for graphical functions. The efficiency of the theory of graphical functions relies on the existence of these
identities as tools for their computation.

The most important technique to calculate graphical functions is appending a single edge
e
of weight
\nu_{e}=1
to the vertex
z
(thus creating a new internal vertex),

(6)

where we associate the labels
0,1,z
to the external vertices
z_{0},z_{1},z_{2}
.

The main result of this article is the algorithm that performs the operation of appending an edge to a given graphical function in arbitrary even dimensions
\geq 4

by taking single-valued primitives, see Section
25
.

The three-point functions associated to
G_{1}
and
G
are related by an inhomogeneous Laplace equation,

(7)

\Delta_{z_{2}}A_{G_{1}}(z_{0},z_{1},z_{2})=-\frac{4}{(\lambda-1)!}A_{G}(z_{0},z_{1},z_{2}).

To verify (
7
), recall that for an edge
e
with weight
\nu_{e}=1
the relevant factor in the integrand of (
3
) is a Green’s functions of the
D
-dimensional Laplacian
[
23
]
,

(8)

\Delta_{u}\frac{1}{Q_{e}(u,v)^{\lambda}}=-\frac{4\pi^{\lambda-1}}{(\lambda-1)!}\delta^{(D)}(u-v),

where
\delta^{(D)}
is the
D
-dimensional Dirac delta distribution.

In Lemma
30
we derive from (
7
) the following relation between the graphical functions
f_{G}(z)
and
f_{G_{1}}(z)
,

(9)

\frac{1}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{\lambda}}\Delta_{\lambda-1}(z-{\overline{z}})^{\lambda}f_{G_{1}}(z)=-\frac{1}{(\lambda-1)!}f_{G}(z),

where
\Delta_{\lambda-1}
is an effective Laplacian acting on complex functions

(10)

\Delta_{\lambda-1}=\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{z}}+\frac{\lambda(\lambda-1)}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{2}}.

Solving the partial differential equation (
9
) for
f_{G_{1}}(z)
is one of the main results of this article.
We proceed in three steps: Firstly, a general solution has to be found. This is trivial for
D=4
,
\lambda=1
, where the effective Laplacian factors.
The general solution in even dimensions
\geq 4
is constructed in Theorem
34
. The proof of Theorem
34
is in Sections
22
and
23
.

As second step towards the solution of (
9
) we need to control the kernel of (
10
). Remarkably, the kernel is trivial in the space of graphical functions:

Theorem 1
.

The differential equation (
9
) has a unique solution in the space of graphical functions.

The general properties of graphical functions in Theorem
5
specify this unique solution.
Formally, Theorem
1
follows from Theorem
36
which will be proved in Section
24
.

With existence and uniqueness of the solution one obtains an algorithm that allows one to calculate the graphical function
f_{G_{1}}(z)
from the graphical function
f_{G}(z)
.
This algorithm is presented in Section
25
as the last step in the solution of (
9
).
It relies on the construction of single-valued primitives in an underlying function space. In general, this is a difficult task. For many practical calculations, however,
the space of generalized single-valued hyperlogarithms (GSVHs) suffices
[
38
]
, see Section
4
. Whenever
f_{G}(z)
is a GSVH, then
f_{G_{1}}(z)
is a GSVH which can be computed
(subject to mild constraints from time and memory consumption). The algorithm of appending an edge has been implemented by the second author in
[
37
]
.

The surprising fact that (
9
) admits a closed solution may be related to the interpretation of the effective
Laplacian (
10
) in terms of the Casimir of a representation of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sl}(2,{\mathbb{C}})
whose eigenvalue is related to the dimension
D
, see Section
6.2
.

The core of the article are proofs of the results in Sections
5
to
15
. Because the proofs require some preparations and rely on independent techniques
they are moved to Sections
16
to
24
.

The article also contains a generalization of the Gegenbauer identity in Section 3.6 of
[
33
]
to even dimensions
\geq 4
, see Section
8
.
The proof of the classical four-dimensional case is in Section
21
.

2.
Graphical functions

2.1.
Definition

It is convenient to define graphical functions as position space Feynman integrals (
3
) evaluated at specific vectors.

Definition 2
.

Let
z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\{0,1\}
with real part
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Re}z=(z+{\overline{z}})/2
and imaginary part
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Im}z=(z-{\overline{z}})/2\mathrm{i}
. Define the three vectors
z_{0},z_{1},z_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
as

(11)

z_{0}=0=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
0\\
0\\
\vdots\\
0\end{array}\right),\quad z_{1}=e_{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
0\\
0\\
\vdots\\
0\end{array}\right),\quad z_{2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Re}z\\
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Im}z\\
0\\
\vdots\\
0\end{array}\right).

Let
\lambda\in\{1,2,3,\ldots\}
and
G
be a weighted graph with external vertices
z_{0},z_{1},z_{2}
such that the three-point function
A_{G}(z_{0},z_{1},z_{2})
in (
3
) exists. Then
(compare (
5
) with
\|z_{1}-z_{0}\|=\|e_{1}\|=1
)

(12)

f_{G}^{(\lambda)}(z)=A_{G}(0,e_{1},z_{2}(z))

is the graphical function of
G
in
D=2\lambda+2
dimensions at the complex value
z
. In the graph
G
we use labels 0, 1,
z
for
z_{0}
,
z_{1}
,
z_{2}
with the identification illustrated
in Figure
1
. We drop the superscript
(\lambda)
if we do not need to refer to the dimension of the ambient space.

Definition
2
provides an explicit formula for
f_{G}^{(\lambda)}(z)
which is equivalent to (
5
).

A non-trivial graphical function is never holomorphic in
z
but a function of
z
and its complex conjugate
{\overline{z}}
. Note that in some situations it can be convenient to consider
z

and
{\overline{z}}
as independent analytic variables. This perspective is essential in QFT for the transition from Euclidean to Minkowski metric where
z
and
{\overline{z}}
are independent real variables.

Occasionally we identify
f_{G}(z)
with the graph
G
together with the external labels
0,1,z
. In this case we write
G_{0,1,z}
for the graph
G
.

Example 3
.

Let
\emptyset
be the empty graph (with no edges) and
uv
be the graph with single edge
uv
of weight
\nu_{uv}
between the external vertices
u,v\in\{0,1,z\}
. We get

f_{\emptyset}(z)=f_{01}(z)=1,\quad f_{0z}(z)=(z{\overline{z}})^{-\lambda\nu_{0z}},\quad f_{1z}(z)=[(z-1)({\overline{z}}-1)]^{-\lambda\nu_{1z}}.

The first non-trivial graphical function is associated to the three-star
.

where

(14)

D(z)=\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Im}(\mathrm{Li}\,_{2}(z)+\ln(1-z)\ln|z|)

is the Bloch-Wigner dilogarithm (see e.g.
[
43
]
). The graphical function of the three-star is rational in any even dimension
\geq 6
.
This can be seen by using the integral representation (3.16) in
[
33
]
,

f_{\hbox{\hbox to7.57pt{\vbox to6.77pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.78291pt\lower-2.52219pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\ignorespaces}\ignorespaces{\ignorespaces}\ignorespaces

{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 4.13 -2.38}{fill:none} \ignorespaces

and proving that the residues of the integrand at
t=1/z
and at
t=1/{\overline{z}}
vanish. We, e.g., get for the residue at
t=1/z

\frac{1}{(-z)^{\lambda}}\left.\partial^{\lambda-1}_{t}\right|_{t=z^{-1}}\frac{(z{\overline{z}})^{1-\lambda}-t^{2\lambda-2}}{(1-t{\overline{z}})^{\lambda}}=\left.\frac{(-1)^{\lambda}(2\lambda-2)!(1-(tz)^{\lambda-1})}{(\lambda-1)!z^{2\lambda-1}(1-t{\overline{z}})^{2\lambda-1}}\right|_{t=z^{-1}}=0.

2.2.
Fundamental properties

We need the following generalization of the edge-weight to subgraphs: Let
g
be a subgraph of
G
, then

(15)

N_{g}=\Big(\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{g}}\nu_{e}\Big)-\frac{\lambda+1}{\lambda}|\mathcal{V}_{g}^{\text{int}}|

is the weight of the graph
g
. Note that
N_{g}
is related to the superficial degree of divergence in QFT
[
23
]
.
Equation (
5
) becomes

(16)

A_{G}(z_{0},z_{1},z_{2})=\|z_{1}-z_{0}\|^{-2\lambda N_{G}}f_{G}^{(\lambda)}(z),

subject to the relation (
4
) between
z
and
z_{0},z_{1},z_{2}
.

Following
[
38
]
we define
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}
as the space of functions on
{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\{0,1\}}
which are analytic in
z
and
{\overline{z}}
and
have single-valued log-Laurent expansions (
18
) and (
20
) at 0, 1, and
\infty
(see Proposition 3.16 in
[
33
]
and Theorem 4.4 in
[
35
]
).
Graphical functions have the following three fundamental properties (note that (G1) and (G2) also hold in odd dimensions):

Theorem 5
.

Let
G
be a weighted graph such that the graphical function
f_{G}(z)
exists in
D=2\lambda+2\geq 3
(odd or even) dimensions.
For any vertex set
\mathcal{V}
we define
G[\mathcal{V}]
as the graph which is induced in
G
by
\mathcal{V}
(i.e.
G[\mathcal{V}]
has the edges of
G
which have both endpoints in
\mathcal{V}
).
Then

(G1)

f_{G}(z)
is a symmetric function,
f_{G}(z)=f_{G}({\overline{z}})
.

(G2)

f_{G}(z)
is a single-valued positive real-analytic function on
{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{0,1\}
.

(G3)

f_{G}(z)\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}
, i.e. it admits single-valued log-Laurent expansions at
0
,
1
, and
\infty
, if

(17)

D=4\qquad\text{and}\qquad\nu_{e}=1\text{ for all }e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}.

Explicitly, let
\nu_{z}
(
\nu_{z}^{>}
,
\nu_{z}^{<}
) be the sum of (positive, negative) weights adjacent to the vertex
z
.
At the singular points
s={\overline{s}}=0,1
there exist coefficients
c_{\ell,m,{\overline{m}}}^{s}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that

(18)

f_{G}(z)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{|{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}|}\sum_{m,{\overline{m}}=M_{s}}^{\infty}c_{\ell,m,{\overline{m}}}^{s}[\log(z-s)({\overline{z}}-{\overline{s}})]^{\ell}(z-s)^{m}({\overline{z}}-{\overline{s}})^{\overline{m}}\quad\text{if }|z-s|<1,

where

(19)

M_{s}=-\max_{\mathcal{V}\subseteq{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}}\lambda N_{G[\mathcal{V}\cup\{s,z\}]}\geq\min\{1-\lambda\nu_{z}^{>},-\lambda\nu_{sz}\}.

At infinity there exist coefficients
c_{\ell,m,{\overline{m}}}^{\infty}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that

(20)

f_{G}(z)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{|{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}|}\sum_{m,{\overline{m}}=-\infty}^{M_{\infty}}c_{\ell,m,{\overline{m}}}^{\infty}(\log z{\overline{z}})^{\ell}z^{m}{\overline{z}}^{\overline{m}}\quad\text{if }|z|>1,

where

(21)

M_{\infty}=-\lambda N_{G}+\max_{\mathcal{V}\subseteq{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}}\lambda N_{G[\mathcal{V}\cup\{0,1\}]}\leq\max\{-1-\lambda\nu_{z}^{<},-\lambda\nu_{z}\}.

Property (G1) is implied by (
4
) and (
5
). Property (G2) is proved in
[
21
]
.
Property (G3) is proved in Sections
16
–
20
using Gegenbauer expansions and the concept of radial and angular graphical functions.

Conjecture 6
.

Statement (G3) in Theorem
5
remains valid if Condition (
17
) is weakened to

D=2\lambda+2\geq 4\text{ is even}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lambda\nu_{e}\in{\mathbb{Z}}\text{ for all }e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}.

It is proved in Section
20
that Conjecture
6
follows from Conjecture
86
which posits that one can interchange
the sum in the Gegenbauer expansion with the position space integrals.

Another strategy to prove Conjecture
6
is to shift the dimension
D
. It is known that Feynman integrals in dimension
D
can be expressed as linear combinations of Feynman integrals
in dimension
D-2

[
41
,
27
]
. This suggests that the Conjecture might follow for all even dimensions from the result for
D=4
in Theorem
5
.
Unfortunately, such arguments not only require the existence of the Feynman integral in integer dimensions, but also the existence of Laurent expansions in a small parameter
\varepsilon
in
the vicinity of an integer dimension. The Laurent expansion of a Feynman integral in, for instance,
6-2\varepsilon
dimensions may be expressed in terms of a linear combination
(with
\varepsilon
-dependent coefficients) of
4-2\varepsilon
dimensional Feynman integrals. It is possible to calculate the
\varepsilon
expansion of graphical functions in such
non-integer valued dimensions
[
35
,
39
]
. Unfortunately, rigorous statements such as Theorem
5
on these
\varepsilon
expansions are rare and the whole theory stands
on a much more conjectural basis. This strategy to prove Conjecture
6
would therefore likely require further advances in this framework of dimensionally regularized graphical functions.
An advantage of the dimensional shift method would be that it is not limited to edge weights
1
.

A positive answer to Question
83
also leads to a (possibly partial) proof of the conjecture.
In any case, a proof of Conjecture
6
requires methods in representation theory or in analysis which are beyond the scope of this article.
An alternative approach to a proof could be the parametric representation in Section
2.5
.

There also exist generalizations of (G3) to weights
\lambda\nu_{e}\notin{\mathbb{Z}}
and an analog of (G3) for odd dimensions (with square roots).

Corollary 7
.

If a graphical function
f_{G}(z)
exists in a neighborhood of one value
z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\{0,1\}
, then it exists for all
z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\{0,1\}
.

Proof.

This follows from (G2) by real-analytic continuation.
∎

The existence of a log-Laurent expansion at infinity promotes graphical functions to objects on the punctured Riemann sphere.
Although (G3) is of technical nature it is an important property for the theory of graphical functions, see e.g. Section
24
. Single-valued log-Laurent expansions
are also central in the theory of GSVHs which is vital for computations with graphical functions
[
38
]
.

2.3.
Completion

The weighted analog of the vertex degree (the number of edges attached to a given vertex) is the sum of the weights of all edges incident to the vertex (the vertex weight).

On the Riemann sphere it is natural to consider conformal transformations. We prepare the use of conformal symmetry by adding a fourth external vertex
\infty
to the graph.
This vertex
\infty
connects to all internal vertices such that their weights become
2(\lambda+1)/\lambda
.

Definition 8
.

Let
G
be a graph with weighted edges and three external vertices
0,1,z
. A completion
{\overline{G}}
of
G
in
D=2\lambda+2
dimensions is a graph
G
with an extra external vertex
\infty
and
weighted edges
\mathcal{E}_{G}\cup\{01\}\cup\{x\infty:x\in\mathcal{V}_{G}\}
such that every internal vertex has weight
2(\lambda+1)/\lambda
and every external vertex has weight zero.

Any graph which is completed up to edges between external vertices (i.e. all internal vertices have weight
2(\lambda+1)/\lambda
) is internally completed.

The de-completion of a completed graphical function
{\overline{G}}
is
{\overline{G}}\backslash\{\infty\}
. It may differ from
G
by an extra edge between the external vertices 0 and 1.
Because this edge has quadric
Q_{e}=1
in (
2
) it does not contribute to the graphical function,

(22)

f_{{\overline{G}}}(z)\equiv f_{{\overline{G}}\backslash\{\infty\}}(z)=f_{G}(z).

Because only completed graphs have a vertex
\infty
, we can use the same notation for graphical functions of completed and uncompleted graphs.

Proposition 9

(Lemma 3.18 in
[
33
]
)
.

Completion is always possible and unique. If
G
has weights in
\frac{1}{\lambda}{\mathbb{Z}}
, then
{\overline{G}}
also has weights in
\frac{1}{\lambda}{\mathbb{Z}}
. A completed graph
{\overline{G}}
has total weight
N_{\overline{G}}=0

(see (
15
)).

Proof.

Most of the proof is in
[
33
]
. Because of the importance of completion we reproduce the proof.

Firstly, we internally complete the graph
G
by adding
\infty
and weighted edges from
\infty
to internal vertices such that all internal vertices have weight
2(\lambda+1)/\lambda
.
We denote the internally completed graph with
G^{\prime}
. It is clear that internal completion is always possible.
Let
\nu_{0}
,
\nu_{1}
,
\nu_{z}
,
\nu_{\infty}
be the total weights of the external vertices in
G^{\prime}
.

Next, we add an edge
z\infty
of weight
-\nu_{z}
to
G^{\prime}
. This gives
z
the total weight zero whereas the weight of
\infty
becomes
\nu_{\infty}-\nu_{z}
.

We are left with the triangle
01
,
0\infty
,
1\infty
to be added to the graph in order to nullify the total weights of
0,1,\infty
.
The weights are given by the unique solution of a linear system,

(23)

\nu_{01}=\frac{-\nu_{0}-\nu_{1}-\nu_{z}+\nu_{\infty}}{2},\quad\nu_{0\infty}=\frac{-\nu_{0}+\nu_{1}+\nu_{z}-\nu_{\infty}}{2},\quad\nu_{1\infty}=\frac{\nu_{0}-\nu_{1}+\nu_{z}-\nu_{\infty}}{2}.

We now assume that all weights of
G
are in
\frac{1}{\lambda}{\mathbb{Z}}
. Because the total weights of internal vertices in
G
are in
\frac{1}{\lambda}{\mathbb{Z}}
, all weights of
G^{\prime}

are in
\frac{1}{\lambda}{\mathbb{Z}}
. We also have
\nu_{0},\nu_{1},\nu_{z},\nu_{\infty}\in\frac{1}{\lambda}{\mathbb{Z}}
. Therefore
\nu_{z\infty}\in\frac{1}{\lambda}{\mathbb{Z}}
.
To show that
\nu_{01},\nu_{0\infty},\nu_{1\infty}\in\frac{1}{\lambda}{\mathbb{Z}}
we sum the weights of half-edges in
G^{\prime}
and obtain

(24)

\frac{2(\lambda+1)}{\lambda}|\mathcal{V}_{G^{\prime}}^{\text{int}}|+\nu_{0}+\nu_{1}+\nu_{z}+\nu_{\infty}=2\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G^{\prime}}}\nu_{e}\in\frac{2}{\lambda}{\mathbb{Z}}.

Hence
\nu_{\text{ext}}=\nu_{0}+\nu_{1}+\nu_{z}+\nu_{\infty}\in\frac{2}{\lambda}{\mathbb{Z}}
and
\nu_{01}=\nu_{\infty}-\frac{1}{2}\nu_{\text{ext}}
,
\nu_{0\infty}=\nu_{1}+\nu_{z}-\frac{1}{2}\nu_{\text{ext}}
,

\nu_{1\infty}=\nu_{0}+\nu_{z}-\frac{1}{2}\nu_{\text{ext}}\in\frac{1}{\lambda}{\mathbb{Z}}
.

With (
23
) the total weight of the completion is
N_{G^{\prime}}-\nu_{z}+\nu_{01}+\nu_{0\infty}+\nu_{1\infty}=N_{G^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{2}\nu_{\text{ext}}
which vanishes by (
24
).
∎

Example 10
.

The completion of the three-star is depicted in Figure
2
.
In Figure
3
the completions of the graphs
G_{a}
and
G_{b}
are depicted.

By conformal symmetry a convergent Feynman integral of an (internally) completed graph
{\overline{G}}
with four external vertices
A_{\overline{G}}(z_{0},z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})
can be expressed in two
cross-ratios (compare (
4
))

(25)

\frac{\|z_{2}-z_{0}\|^{2}\|z_{3}-z_{1}\|^{2}}{\|z_{1}-z_{0}\|^{2}\|z_{3}-z_{2}\|^{2}}=z{\overline{z}},\quad\frac{\|z_{2}-z_{1}\|^{2}\|z_{3}-z_{0}\|^{2}}{\|z_{1}-z_{0}\|^{2}\|z_{3}-z_{2}\|^{2}}=(z-1)({\overline{z}}-1).

Therefore the theory of graphical functions also covers convergent conformal Feynman integrals which are prominent in super Yang-Mills Theories, see e.g.
[
19
]
.

We will see in Section
2.4
that a completed graphical function is invariant under a transformation of external labels which stabilizes a certain cross-ratio.
We can also use completion to formulate a combinatorial criterion for the existence of a graphical function.

Proposition 11

(Lemma 3.19 in
[
33
]
)
.

The graphical function
f_{\overline{G}}(z)
exists (for all
z\in{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\{0,1\}
) if and only if (see (
15
))

(26)

N_{{\overline{G}}[\mathcal{V}]}<(|\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}|-1)\frac{\lambda+1}{\lambda}

for all
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{V}_{G}
with
|\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{int}}|\geq 1
internal and
|\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{ext}}|\leq 1
external vertices.

Proof.

The proof is by power-counting
[
28
]
. It is the weighted analog of the proof of Lemma 3.19 in
[
33
]
(the formulation of Lemma 3.19 in
[
33
]
is slightly imprecise,
see also
[
21
]
).
∎

Note that it suffices to apply (
26
) to internally completed graphs. If
G
fails to be convergent for some
\mathcal{V}
, then
G
is ultraviolet divergent if
\infty\notin\mathcal{V}

(Example
12
) and infrared divergent if
\infty\in\mathcal{V}
(Example
13
).

Example 12
.

Let
G
be a graph with an edge
e=uv
of weight
\nu_{e}=(\lambda+1)/\lambda
. We assume that not both
u
and
v
are external and set
\mathcal{V}=\{u,v\}
in (
26
).
If
u
or
v
is external, then
N_{{\overline{G}}[\mathcal{V}]}=0
. If both
u
and
v
are internal, then
N_{{\overline{G}}[\mathcal{V}]}=-(\lambda+1)/\lambda
. In both cases we conclude that the graphical
function
f_{G}(z)
is (ultraviolet) divergent.

Example 13
.

Let
G
be a graph with an internal vertex
v
of total weight
(\lambda+1)/\lambda
. In the completed graph
{\overline{G}}
we have
\nu_{v\infty}=(\lambda+1)/\lambda
.
With
\mathcal{V}=\{0,\infty\}
the graphical function
f_{G}(z)
is (infrared) divergent.

2.4.
Permutation of external vertices

By Theorem
5
graphical functions live on the Riemann sphere with punctures at
0,1,\infty
. Let

(27)

\mathcal{M}_{01\infty}=\Big\{z\mapsto\phi(z)\in\Big\{z,1-z,\frac{z-1}{z},\frac{z}{z-1},\frac{1}{1-z},\frac{1}{z}\Big\}\Big\}

be the permutation group of these punctures by Möbius transformations. Consider a graphical function
f_{G}(z)
with external labels
0,1,z
and
\phi\in\mathcal{M}_{01\infty}
.
We define the graphical function of the graph
G^{\prime}
with external labels
0,1,\phi(z)
as
f_{G^{\prime}}(z)=f_{G}(\phi(z))
and lift this definition to completed graphs.
Two completed graphical functions which differ only in external labels are equal if the cross-ratios of the external vertices are equal. In other words, a permutation of external vertices
changes the argument of a completed graphical function by a Möbius transformation in
\mathcal{M}_{01\infty}
. Extending the symmetry from the six-fold permutation of
0,1,z
to the 24-fold
permutation of
0,1,z,\infty
is the main benefit of completion.

Theorem 14

(Theorem 3.20 in
[
33
]
)
.

Let
{\overline{G}}_{1}
and
{\overline{G}}_{2}
be two completed graphical functions which differ only in their external labels
z_{0}^{i},z_{1}^{i},z_{2}^{i},z_{3}^{i}\in\mathcal{V}_{{\overline{G}}_{i}}^{\mathrm{ext}}
,

z^{i}_{j}\in\{0,1,\phi_{i}(z),\infty\}
,
\phi_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{01\infty}
. If the two sets of external labels have equal cross-ratios of complex numbers

(28)

\frac{(z^{1}_{2}-z^{1}_{0})(z^{1}_{3}-z^{1}_{1})}{(z^{1}_{2}-z^{1}_{1})(z^{1}_{3}-z^{1}_{0})}=\frac{(z^{2}_{2}-z^{2}_{0})(z^{2}_{3}-z^{2}_{1})}{(z^{2}_{2}-z^{2}_{1})(z^{2}_{3}-z^{2}_{0})},

then

(29)

f_{{\overline{G}}_{1}}(z)=f_{{\overline{G}}_{2}}(z).

In particular, every completed graphical function is invariant under a double transposition of external labels.

Proof.

The symmetry under permutation of
0,1,z
is evident from the definition of graphical functions by invariants (
4
) and
N_{{\overline{G}}_{1}}=N_{{\overline{G}}_{2}}=0
by Proposition
9
.
The general result follows from the conformal symmetry of the integrand in (
3
). The proof is in
[
33
]
.
∎

0
1
z
G_{a}
0
1
z
\infty
{\overline{G}}_{a}
-3
-2
1
0
\infty
z
{\overline{G}}_{b}
-3
-2
1
0
z
G_{b}
-3
1
0
G_{c}
{\overline{G}}_{c}=K_{5}

Figure 3.
The calculation of the four-dimensional graphical function
G_{a}
by completion. Edge-weights
\neq 1
are indicated. We complete to
{\overline{G}}_{a}
, double transpose
the external vertices to
{\overline{G}}_{b}
, de-complete to
G_{b}
, remove edges between external vertices to
G_{c}
, and period complete to
{\overline{G}}_{c}
.

Example 15

(Example 3.21 in
[
33
]
)
.

Consider the four-dimensional graphical function associated to
G_{a}
in Figure
3
. The completion
{\overline{G}}_{a}
becomes
{\overline{G}}_{b}
by a double transposition of external vertices.
The graph
{\overline{G}}_{b}
de-completes to
G_{b}
. We obtain

f_{G_{a}}(z)=f_{{\overline{G}}_{a}}(z)=f_{{\overline{G}}_{b}}(z)=f_{G_{b}}(z).

The graphical function
f_{G_{b}}(z)
can easily be calculated, see Examples
23
and
28
.

Internally completed graphs with no edges between external labels and no distinction of external labels are equivalence classes of graphical functions with equal complexity.
The graphs in Example
3
are all in the equivalence class of the empty graph.

2.5.
Parametric representation

The position space definition of a graphical function in (
3
) and (
12
) can be replaced by a parametric representation.
The virtue of the parametric representation is that the dimension
D
enters only as a parameter so that the generalization to
D\in{\mathbb{C}}
is possible.
Moreover, there exists the method of parametric integration by F. Brown and E. Panzer
[
10
,
11
,
29
]
, see Section
14
.

We follow
[
21
]
for the subsequent definition and theorem.

Definition 16
.

Let
P=\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{N}\}
denote a partition of the external vertices
\{0,1,z\}
of a graph
G
.
We write
\mathcal{F}_{G}^{P}
for the set of all spanning forests
T_{1}\cup\ldots\cup T_{N}
consisting of exactly
N
(pairwise disjoint) trees
T_{i}
such that
P_{i}\subseteq T_{i}
.
The
dual spanning forest polynomial
associated to
P
is

(30)

\widetilde{\Psi}_{G}^{P}(\alpha)=\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}_{G}^{P}}\;\prod_{e\in F}\alpha_{e}.

We write
\widetilde{\Psi}_{G}
for
\widetilde{\Psi}_{G}^{0,1,z}
and define the polynomial

(31)

\widetilde{\Phi}_{G}(\alpha,z)=\sum_{abc\in\{01z,0z1,1z0\}}|a-b|^{2}\widetilde{\Psi}_{G}^{ab,c}(\alpha).

.

2
with edges
1,2,3
attached to vertices

Theorem 18
.

Let
G
be a non-empty weighted graph such that the graphical function
f_{G}(z)
exists. We label the edges by
1,2,\ldots,|\mathcal{E}_{G}|
.
For any set of non-negative integers
n_{e}
such that
n_{e}+\lambda\nu_{e}>0
for all edges
e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}
we have the following
dual parametric representation

of
f_{G}(z)
as the convergent projective integral (
\Gamma(x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{x-1}\exp(-t)\mathrm{d}t
is the gamma function)

(33)

f_{G}(z)=\frac{(-1)^{\sum_{e}\!n_{e}}\,\Gamma(\lambda N_{G})}{\prod_{e}\Gamma(n_{e}+\lambda\nu_{e})}\int_{\Delta}\Omega\Big[\prod_{e}\alpha_{e}^{n_{e}+\lambda\nu_{e}-1}\partial_{\alpha_{e}}^{n_{e}}\Big]\frac{1}{\widetilde{\Phi}_{G}^{\lambda N_{G}}\widetilde{\Psi}_{G}^{\lambda+1-\lambda N_{G}}},

where

(34)

\Omega=\sum_{e=1}^{|\mathcal{E}_{G}|}(-1)^{e-1}\alpha_{e}\mathrm{d}\alpha_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\widehat{\mathrm{d}\alpha_{e}}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathrm{d}\alpha_{|\mathcal{E}_{G}|}

is the projective volume form. The integration domain is given by the positive coordinate simplex

(35)

\Delta=\{(\alpha_{1}:\alpha_{2}:\ldots:\alpha_{|\mathcal{E}_{G}|})\colon\alpha_{e}>0\text{ for all }e\in\{1,2,\ldots,|\mathcal{E}_{G}|\}\}\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{|\mathcal{E}_{G}|-1}{\mathbb{R}}.

If all weights
\nu_{e}
are positive, the parametric representation can be dualized by a Cremona transformation, see Corollary 1.8 in
[
21
]
.

3.
Periods

Feynman periods are constant graphical functions. If a graph
G
has an isolated vertex
z
, then the integrand in the corresponding integral (
12
)
has no dependence on
z
. The Feynman integral depends on the sole invariant
\|z_{1}-z_{0}\|
which is 1 in the context of graphical functions.
A Feynman period is hence given by a graph with two external vertices 0 and 1, see e.g.
G_{c}
in Figure
3
.

Feynman periods can be calculated by specializing graphical functions to
z=0
,
z=1
, or
z=\infty
, see e.g.
[
15
]
.
A more powerful method is to choose a convenient internal vertex in a period graph
G
and promote it to the external vertex
z
. If the corresponding
graphical function can be calculated, the period can be obtained by integration of
z
over
{\mathbb{C}}
.

Definition 19
.

Let
G
be a graph with two external labels
0,1
. The Feynman period
P_{G}
is the constant graphical function
f_{G\cup\{z\}}
with an isolated external vertex
z
.

A combinatorial criterion for convergence of the Feynman period
P_{G}
can be obtained from the corresponding result for the graphical function
f_{G\cup\{z\}}
, see Proposition
11
.

Proposition 20

(Lemma 3.34 in
[
33
]
)
.

Let
G
be a graph with two external labels 0 and 1 such that the Feynman period
P_{G}
exists. In
G^{\prime}
we promote one internal vertex of
G

to the external vertex
z
. Then (
\mathrm{d}^{2}z=\mathrm{d}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Re}z\wedge\mathrm{d}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Im}z
)

(36)

P_{G}=(-1)^{\lambda}\frac{(\lambda-1)!}{(2\lambda-1)!}\int_{\mathbb{C}}(z-{\overline{z}})^{2\lambda}f_{G^{\prime}}(z)\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}z}{2\pi}.

Proof.

The proof uses spherical coordinates (
81
). The volume measure
Z^{D-1}\sin^{D-2}\phi^{z}_{1}\mathrm{d}Z\mathrm{d}\phi^{z}_{1}
translates into
(-4)^{-\lambda}(z-{\overline{z}})^{2\lambda}\mathrm{d}^{2}z/2
.
The pre-factor originates from the volume of the
(D-2)
-sphere, see
[
33
]
.
∎

For the evaluation of the integral over the complex plane we can take advantage of the single-valuedness of graphical functions
and use a residue theorem in
[
33
]
(Theorem 2.29).

Like graphical functions, Feynman periods have a completion
[
34
]
.

Definition 21
.

Let
G
be a graph with weighted edges and two external vertices
0,1
. The period completion
{\overline{G}}
of
G
is the graph obtained from the completion of the graphical function

G\cup\{z\}
(with isolated vertex
z
, Definition
8
) and the additional triangle
01
,
0\infty
,
1\infty
with edge-weights
(\lambda+1)/\lambda
.
In
{\overline{G}}
we delete
z
and forget the labels
0,1,\infty
so that
{\overline{G}}
is an unlabeled weighted
2(\lambda+1)/\lambda
-regular graph.

It is clear by Proposition
9
that the completed period has weights in
\frac{1}{\lambda}{\mathbb{Z}}
if the uncompleted graph has weights in
\frac{1}{\lambda}{\mathbb{Z}}
.
The period is invariant under completion.

Theorem 22

(Theorem 2.7 in
[
34
]
)
.

Let
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
be two graphs with external vertices
0,1
and equal period completion
{\overline{G}}
. Then

(37)

P_{\overline{G}}\equiv P_{G_{1}}=P_{G_{2}}.

Proof.

The proof in
[
34
]
relies on conformal transformations of the integrand.
∎

Completion is a means to handle equivalence classes of graphs with equal Feynman period. Note that completion of periods is more powerful than completion of graphical functions.
One has the freedom to remove any vertex
\infty
from the completed graph and thereafter pick any two vertices 0 and 1 for the calculation of the period.

1
2
3
4
5
6
n
\bullet\bullet\bullet
0

1
2
3
4
5
6
n
\bullet\bullet\bullet
0
\infty

Figure 4.
The wheel with
n
spokes
W\!S_{n,D}
in
D=2\lambda+2
dimensions and its completion
\overline{W\!S}_{n,D}
.
The solid edges on the rim

have weight
1
,
the dashed spokes

have weight
1/\lambda
and the
dotted edge

between
0
and
\infty
in
\overline{W\!S}_{n,D}
has weight
(D-n)/\lambda
.

Example 23
.

The graph
G_{c}
in Figure
3
completes to
K_{5}
, the complete graph with five vertices. In four dimensions its period is
[
17
]

P_{K_{5}}=6\zeta(3),

where
\zeta
is the Riemann zeta function.

Example 24

(Complete graphs
\overline{K}_{n,D}
)
.

We generalize Example
23
to complete graphs
\overline{K}_{n,D}
of uniform weight
\nu
in
D
dimensions. By
2(\lambda+1)/\lambda
-regularity the weight of all edges is

\nu=\frac{D}{(n-1)\lambda}.

The period of the graph
\overline{K}_{3,D}
is 1.

The period of the graph
\overline{K}_{4,D}
is given by (
51
),

P_{\overline{K}_{4,D}}=\frac{\Gamma(D/6)^{3}}{\Gamma(D/3)^{3}}.

It is rational for
D\in 6{\mathbb{Z}}
where the weight is in
\frac{1}{\lambda}{\mathbb{Z}}
.

For
n\geq 5
we impose the constraint
(n-1)|D
so that
\lambda\nu\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. The graph
\overline{K}_{5,D}
is constructible (see Section
7
).
By Conjecture
46
we expect that the period is a multiple zeta value (MZV) of weight
\leq 3
,
i.e. a rational linear combination of 1 and
\zeta(3)
. With
HyperlogProcedures

[
37
]
we get

\displaystyle P_{\overline{K}_{5,4}}

\displaystyle=6\zeta(3)\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\text{(see Example \ref{exf32}),}

\displaystyle P_{\overline{K}_{5,8}}

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{5}\zeta(3)-\frac{7}{10},

\displaystyle P_{\overline{K}_{5,12}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{80}\zeta(3)-\frac{173}{11520},

\displaystyle P_{\overline{K}_{5,16}}

\displaystyle=\frac{47}{617760}\zeta(3)-\frac{73219}{800616960},

\displaystyle P_{\overline{K}_{5,20}}

\displaystyle=\frac{79}{448081920}\zeta(3)-\frac{984571}{4645713346560}.

Because the numerical evaluation of
P_{\overline{K}_{5,D}}
becomes very small for large
D
, periods of complete graphs with five vertices provide a sequence of rational approximations of
\zeta(3)
.

Beyond five vertices complete graphs are not constructible. For six vertices, double use of unique triangle reductions, see Section
11
and Example
59
, gives
[
37
]

\displaystyle P_{\overline{K}_{6,10}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{5}{7}\zeta(5)+\frac{19}{30}\zeta(3)-\frac{13}{630},

\displaystyle P_{\overline{K}_{6,20}}

\displaystyle=\frac{25}{5444195328}\zeta(5)-\frac{3886573}{1440534083788800}\zeta(3)-\frac{1417432087}{933466086295142400},

\displaystyle P_{\overline{K}_{6,30}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{109}{3737370802913280000000}\zeta(5)+\frac{3035142067981}{155555277863119316582400000000000}\zeta(3)

\displaystyle\quad+\,\frac{684204291515294677}{100799820055301317145395200000000000000}.

For
n\geq 7
the computation of
\overline{K}_{n,D}
is complicated. With
[
37
]
we get (see Example
61
)

P_{\overline{K}_{7,6}}=360\zeta(3,5)+690\zeta(3)\zeta(5)-\frac{29}{315}\pi^{8},

where
\zeta(3,5)=\sum_{0<k<\ell}k^{-3}\ell^{-5}
is an MZV of weight eight.

Example 25

(Wheels
W\!S_{n,D}
)
.

Another generalization of Example
23
is given by the wheels with
n
spokes in Figure
4

where the spokes have weight
1/\lambda
and the rim has weight 1. In their completion the edge
0\infty
has weight
(D-n)/\lambda
. The period
is convergent if
(D-n)/\lambda\leq 1\Leftrightarrow D\leq 2n-2
(see Example
13
). In Example
87
we will obtain
a closed expression for the period of
W\!S_{n,D}
using radial and angular graphical functions (the Gegenbauer method).

For even
D
the result is a
{\mathbb{Q}}
-linear combination of odd single zetas with weights ranging from
2n-2\lambda-1
to
2n-3
.
The wheels
W\!S_{n,D}
are constructible by appending weight 1 edges, only. The wheel has
n+1
vertices, so that the maximum weight is consistent with Theorem
45
.

For odd
D
the period of
W\!S_{n,D}
is a polynomial in
\pi^{2}
of degree
n-1
.

3.1.
Parametric representation

In Section
2.5
we gave a (dual) parametric representation for graphical functions. As a corollary we obtain an analogous result for periods.
For an unlabeled graph
G
we define the dual graph (Kirchhoff)-polynomial
[
25
]
as (see (
30
))

(38)

\widetilde{\Psi}_{G}=\widetilde{\Psi}_{G}^{\emptyset}=\sum_{T}\prod_{e\in T}\alpha_{e}

where the sum is over spanning trees.

Corollary 26

(Corollary of Theorem
18
)
.

Let
{\overline{G}}
be a weighted
2(\lambda+1)/\lambda
-regular graph such that the Feynman period
P_{\overline{G}}
exists in
D=2\lambda+2\geq 3
dimensions.
Let
\infty\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{G}}
be a vertex in
{\overline{G}}
and
G={\overline{G}}\backslash\{\infty\}
be a de-completion of
{\overline{G}}
.
We label the edges of
G
by
1,2,\ldots,|\mathcal{E}_{G}|
. For any set of non-negative integers
n_{e}
such that
n_{e}+\lambda\nu_{e}>0
for all edges
e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}
we have the following

dual parametric representation
of
P_{G}\equiv P_{\overline{G}}
as the convergent projective integral

(39)

P_{G}=\frac{(-1)^{\sum_{e}\!n_{e}}\,\Gamma(\lambda+1)}{\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}\Gamma(n_{e}+\lambda\nu_{e})}\int_{\Delta}\Omega\Big[\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}\alpha_{e}^{n_{e}+\lambda\nu_{e}-1}\partial_{\alpha_{e}}^{n_{e}}\Big]\frac{1}{\widetilde{\Psi}_{G}^{\lambda+1}},

where
\Omega
is defined in (
34
). The integration domain is given by the positive coordinate simplex (
35
).

Proof.

The theorem is trivial in the case that
G
is a single edge of weight
(\lambda+1)/\lambda
. We may assume that
G
has at least three vertices. Let 0, 1 be two vertices in
G

such that the edge
01
has positive weight
\nu_{01}
(such an edge always exists). We define
G_{0,1,z}=G\cup\{z\}\backslash\{01\}
as the graph which is obtained
from
G
by removing the edge 01 and adding an isolated vertex
z
. The completion of
G_{0,1,z}
has weight
N_{{\overline{G}}_{0,1,z}}=0
, see Proposition
9
.
The graph of the completed period
{\overline{G}}
has the edges of
{\overline{G}}_{0,1,z}
plus three edges of weight
(\lambda+1)/\lambda
, see Definition
21
.
So,
N_{G}=(\lambda+1)/\lambda
if one considers 0 and 1 as external vertices in
G={\overline{G}}\backslash\{\infty\}
. We find

(40)

N_{G_{0,1,z}}=\frac{\lambda+1}{\lambda}-\nu_{01}.

Moreover, we obtain from Definition
16
that

\widetilde{\Psi}_{G}=\alpha_{01}\widetilde{\Psi}_{G_{0,1,z}}+\widetilde{\Phi}_{G_{0,1,z}}.

Using integration by parts it suffices to prove (
39
) for
n_{01}=0
. In an affine coordinate patch where some
\alpha_{e}=1
for
e\neq 01
the integral over
\alpha_{01}

in (
39
) is

\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\alpha_{01}^{\lambda\nu_{01}-1}\mathrm{d}\alpha_{01}}{(\alpha_{01}\widetilde{\Psi}_{G_{0,1,z}}+\widetilde{\Phi}_{G_{0,1,z}})^{\lambda+1}}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{x^{\lambda\nu_{01}-1}\mathrm{d}x}{(x+1)^{\lambda+1}}\,\cdot\,\frac{\widetilde{\Phi}_{G_{0,1,z}}^{\lambda\nu_{01}-\lambda-1}}{\widetilde{\Psi}_{G_{0,1,z}}^{\lambda\nu_{01}}}

where we substituted
\alpha_{01}=\widetilde{\Phi}_{G_{0,1,z}}x/\widetilde{\Psi}_{G_{0,1,z}}
. We write the first factor on the right hand side as projective integral

\int x^{\lambda\nu_{01}-1}y^{\lambda(1-\nu_{01})}/{(x+y)^{\lambda+1}}\Omega
with coordinates
(x:y)
. The affine patch
x+y=1
with
\Omega=\mathrm{d}x
gives
the definition of the Euler beta function. For the integral over
\alpha_{01}
in (
39
) we hence get

\frac{\Gamma(\lambda\nu_{01})\Gamma(\lambda+1-\lambda\nu_{01})}{\Gamma{(\lambda+1})}\frac{1}{\widetilde{\Phi}_{G_{0,1,z}}^{\lambda+1-\lambda\nu_{01}}\widetilde{\Psi}_{G_{0,1,z}}^{\lambda\nu_{01}}}.

With this result and (
40
) Equation (
39
) reduces to (
33
) for
G_{0,1,z}
.
∎

In the case of positive weights
\nu_{e}
a Cremona transformation provides a parametric representation of
P_{G}
with integrand
(\prod_{e}\alpha_{e}^{\lambda(1-\nu_{e})})/\Psi_{G}^{\lambda+1}

where
\Psi_{G}=\sum_{T}\prod_{e\notin T}\alpha_{e}
is the Kirchhoff polynomial.

Remark 27
.

Examples of parametrically represented Feynman periods in various even dimensions are invariant differential forms on complexes of graphs in
[
14
]
.

4.
Single-valued integration and generalized single-valued hyperlogarithms

The theory of graphical functions reduces the calculation of corresponding Feynman integrals to single-valued integrations.

For rational functions it suffices to know that a single-valued primitive of
1/(z-a)
is
\log[(z-a)({\overline{z}}-{\overline{a}})]
for any constant
a\in{\mathbb{C}}
.
Finding single-valued primitives in general (or even proving their existence) is an open problem. One expects that under rather mild conditions single-valued functions have
single-valued primitives. First advances are due to F. Brown in the context of hyperlogarithms which generalize the above example to higher weights
[
8
,
9
]
.
Brown’s approach was the mathematically appealing use of generating functions. It turned out that in the context of graphical functions, generating functions are
unwieldy because they carry the information of all hyperlogarithms up to a certain weight while one is only interested in a few very specific ones.
In
[
33
]
the second author overcame the difficulty by constructing a bootstrap algorithm. The essence of the algorithm is a commutative hexagon which allows one to
reduce the single-valued integration of hyperlogarithms to multi-valued (anti-)integrations and single-valued integrations of lower weights
[
35
]
.

The main problem for using single-valued integration in the context of graphical function, however, was that the function space of single-valued hyperlogarithms is too restrictive.
Only the simplest graphical functions can be expressed in terms of single-valued hyperlogarithms.
It turned out that it is necessary to generalize single-valued hyperlogarithms to include primitives of differential forms
\mathrm{d}z/(az{\overline{z}}+bz+c{\overline{z}}+d)
,
a,b,c,d\in{\mathbb{C}}
.
Examples are the primitives of
\log(z{\overline{z}})/(z-1/{\overline{z}})
or of
D(z)/(z-{\overline{z}})
(where
D
is the Bloch-Wigner dilogarithm (
14
)). The latter example was already studied
in
[
16
]
. The construction of these generalized single-valued hyperlogarithms (GSVHs) also relies on the commutative hexagon but the theory is much more involved
[
38
]
.

In fact, the full theory of GSVHs is almost as comprehensive as the theory of graphical functions. It became the second pillar in the framework that uses graphical functions
for calculations in QFT. (The third pillar is the extension to non-integer dimensions in order to handle Feynman integrals with divergences
[
39
]
.)
The main result of the theory of GSVHs is that in the function space of GSVHs single-valued (anti-)primitives always exist and that they can be constructed very efficiently
(for suitable sets of singularities). As it is not easy to summarize the theory in a few paragraphs we refer the interested reader to
[
38
]
.

There exist graphical functions which are not GSVHs (in four dimensions, e.g., the graph
{\overline{G}}_{8}
in Figure
5
).
For these graphical functions the general theory is still valid but there presently exists no handle on their computation. In Definition
31
we
merely assume the existence of single-valued primitives so that the theory of graphical function extends to any future developments in the theory of single-valued integration.

5.
Elementary identities for graphical functions

In many cases graphical functions can be calculated. A particularly tractable case are graphical functions that emerge from the empty graph by a series of combinatorial
operations: adding edges between external vertices (Section
5.2
), products and factors (Sections
5.1
and
5.3
),
permutation of external vertices (Section
2.4
), and appending an edge to the vertex
z
(Section
6
). In this case the graphical function is
constructible

(see Section
7
) and it can be computed in terms of GSVHs
[
38
]
. A Maple implementation is in
HyperlogProcedures

[
37
]
.

5.1.
Products

Assume the graph of an (internally) completed graphical function
{\overline{G}}
disconnects upon the removal of its external vertices into
{\overline{G}}_{1}
and
{\overline{G}}_{2}
where the removed edges
(adjacent to the removed external vertices) are added to corresponding graphs. Then the Feynman integral (
3
) factors into two disjoint sets of integration variables.
Accordingly, the graphical function
f_{\overline{G}}(z)
factors,

(41)

\displaystyle f_{\overline{G}}(z)

\displaystyle=f_{\overline{G}_{1}}(z)f_{\overline{G}_{2}}(z),

which can be depicted diagrammatically as

0
1
z
\infty
\overline{G}_{1}
\overline{G}_{2}
\overline{G}

5.2.
Edges between external vertices

Edges between external vertices provide rational factors by the product reduction from the previous subsection and Example
3
.

Example 28
.

For the graphical function
f_{G_{b}}(z)
in Figure
3
we obtain in four dimensions

f_{G_{b}}(z)=\frac{f_{G_{c}}(z)}{z{\overline{z}}(z-1)({\overline{z}}-1)}=\frac{P_{K_{5}}}{z{\overline{z}}(z-1)({\overline{z}}-1)}=\frac{6\zeta(3)}{z{\overline{z}}(z-1)({\overline{z}}-1)}.

Note that
f_{G_{c}}(z)
is a constant graphical function, see Section
3
. It equals the Feynman period of the completed graph
K_{5}
, see Example
23
.

5.3.
Period factors

We can interpret Example
28
in the following way: An (internally) completed graphical function with isolated vertex
\infty
evaluates to a Feynman period
times a weighted triangle between the external vertices
0,1,z
. This generalizes to the following proposition.

Proposition 29
.

Let
{\overline{G}}
be an (internally) completed graphical function with a three-vertex-split into
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
.
We include the split vertices
a,b,c
together with their corresponding edges to
G_{1}
or
G_{2}
(respectively) and assume that
G_{1}\backslash\{a,b,c\}
has only internal vertices.
We add (unique) weighted triangles
(ab,ac,bc)
to
G_{1}
and to
G_{2}
to obtain the (unlabeled) completed period
{\overline{G}}_{1}
and the (internally) completed graph
{\overline{G}}_{2}
. Then

(42)

\displaystyle f_{\overline{G}}(z)

\displaystyle=P_{{\overline{G}}_{1}}f_{{\overline{G}}_{2}}(z),

which can be depicted diagrammatically as

{G}_{1}
{G}_{2}
\overline{G}
0
1
z
\infty

{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{25.95276pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}~~.

Proof.

We may add to
{\overline{G}}
a pair of edges
ab
with total weight zero without changing the graphical function
f_{\overline{G}}(z)
.
We move one edge of the pair to
G_{1}
whereas the other one goes to
G_{2}
. Likewise we generate pairs of edges
ac
and
bc
. We can adjust the weights of these pairs such that
the vertices
a
,
b
,
c
have total weight zero in
G_{1}
. This procedure does not affect completion so that the right hand side of (
42
) is insensitive
to the extra edges.

We thus may assume without restriction that the total weights of the vertices
a
,
b
,
c
in
G_{1}
are zero. Hence, the total weights of
a
,
b
,
c
in
{\overline{G}}_{2}
equal
the total weights of
a
,
b
,
c
in the (internally) completed graph
{\overline{G}}
. Therefore,
{\overline{G}}_{2}
is (internally) completed.

Completion adds an isolated vertex
\infty
to
G_{1}
. By Proposition
9
we have
N_{G_{1}}=N_{{\overline{G}}_{1}}=0
and by (
16
) the Feynman integral
A_{G_{1}}
with external
vertices
a
,
b
,
c
equals the graphical function
f_{{\overline{G}}_{1}}(z)
with invariants(
4
). By Theorem
14
we can simultaneously swap the external vertices
0,1
and
z,\infty

without changing the graphical function. Now
z
is an isolated vertex. This implies that the Feynman integral of
G_{1}
is constant and factors from the integral.
By Theorem
22
we have
f_{{\overline{G}}_{1}}(z)=P_{{\overline{G}}_{1}}
yielding (
42
).
∎

If, after the removal of three vertices, a graphical function has a bridge of negative weight
-k/\lambda
,
k=1,2,\ldots
, (and one part is fully internal),
then a period factorization is still possible with a technique that is a straightforward generalization of the method in Section
6.4
.

6.
Appending an edge

In this section we consider graphs which have a single edge
e
with weight
\lambda\nu_{e}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
connecting the external vertex
z
to an internal vertex.
By Theorem
14
this also covers the case that any other external vertex has a single edge.
By convergence we have
\nu_{e}\leq 1
(see Example
12
). We distinguish the cases
\nu_{e}=1
(the main case, Section
6.1
),

0<\nu_{e}<1
(Section
6.3
), and
\nu_{e}<0
(Section
6.4
).

6.1.
Weight
\nu_{e}=1

If a graph
G_{1}
has a single edge
e
of weight
\nu_{e}=1
connecting the vertex
z
to an internal vertex of
G_{1}
,
then the effective Laplace equation (
9
) gives a relation between the graphical functions
f_{G_{1}}(z)
and
f_{G}(z)
in (
6
).

Lemma 30

(Proposition 3.22 in
[
33
]
)
.

In the situation of (
6
) where the edge
e
that is attached to
z
in
G_{1}
has weight
\nu_{e}=1
we have

(43)

\Delta_{\lambda-1}(z-{\overline{z}})^{\lambda}f_{G_{1}}(z)=-\frac{1}{(\lambda-1)!}(z-{\overline{z}})^{\lambda}f_{G}(z)

with the effective Laplacian
\Delta_{\lambda-1}=\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{z}}+\lambda(\lambda-1)/(z-{\overline{z}})^{2}
.

Proof.

The proof uses spherical coordinates (
81
), see
[
33
]
.
∎

We solve the differential equation in three steps: Firstly, we construct a general solution in the space of single-valued functions (Theorem
34
).
Then, we prove in Theorem
36
that the solution is unique in the space of functions with general property (G3) of graphical functions (Theorem
5
).
Finally, we give an algorithm in Section
25
that picks the unique graphical function in the general solution of (
43
).

With the solution of (
43
) we construct the graphical function
G_{1}
from
G
. In practice, we need to work in a function space where single-valued primitives can
be computed. A good such function space are generalized single-valued hyperlogarithms (GSVHs)
[
38
]
.
If
f_{G}(z)
is a GSVH, then also
f_{G_{1}}(z)
is a GSVH, so that the algorithm can be iterated.

The algorithm of appending an edge is the essence of the theory of graphical functions. It is this highly non-trivial construction that makes so many graphical functions
calculable. With the rare exception of the Gegenbauer method in Section
8
, appending an edge is the only situation where actual computations in terms of single-valued
integrations are performed. All other identities in Sections
9
to
12
have the purpose to reduce the calculation of a graphical function to a situation
where an edge can be appended to a simpler graph. Note that the algorithm of appending an edge extends to non-integer dimensions in the context of dimensional regularization
[
35
,
39
]
.

Definition 31
.

The single-valued integral
\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}z
(or
\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}{\overline{z}}
) with respect to
z
(or
{\overline{z}}
) is a fixed vector space endomorphism of
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}
such that

\partial_{z}\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}z=\partial_{\overline{z}}\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}{\overline{z}}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}}
.
If single-valued integrals do not exist on all of
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}
, we restrict ourselves to a maximum subspace on which single-valued integrals exist, see Section
4
.
Such a function space always contains the space of GSVHs
[
38
]
. If a restriction is necessary, the subsequent results inherit this restriction.

Remark 32
.

(1)

Definition
31
does not uniquely specify
\int_{\mathrm{sv}}
. Single-valued integration with respect to
z
, e.g., is only defined up to the addition of rational functions in
{\overline{z}}

with poles at
0
and
1
. For solving (
43
) we can use any version of
\int_{\mathrm{sv}}
.

(2)

In
[
38
]
efficient algorithms for single-valued integration of GSVHs with respect to
z
and
{\overline{z}}
are presented.

(3)

The single-valued integration of GSVHs in
[
37
]
can produce anti-holomorphic poles in
{\mathbb{C}}[({\overline{z}}-{\overline{s}})^{-1}]
for
{\overline{s}}\neq 0,1
.
These poles need to be subtracted to obtain an endomorphism of
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}
.

(4)

It is natural to assume that single-valued integration in the sense of Definition
31
always exist. However, it is unclear how to construct single-valued primitives
for functions which are not GSVHs. By lack of a general construction, solving (
43
) (and hence appending edges) is practically limited to GSVHs.

For
\lambda-1=n=0,1,2,\ldots
we define the following integral operators

\displaystyle I^{+}_{n}:(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}},\quad I^{-}_{n}:\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}\rightarrow(z-{\overline{z}})^{-n}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}},

(44)

\displaystyle f(z)\mapsto I^{\pm}_{n}f(z)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{n-k}\frac{(n+k)!}{(n-k)!k!^{2}}(z-{\overline{z}})^{\pm k}\int_{\mathrm{sv}}(z-{\overline{z}})^{\mp k}f(z)\mathrm{d}z.

Moreover, we define the differential operators
D_{0}=0
,

\displaystyle D_{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k-1}\frac{(n-k)!}{(n+k)!}(z-{\overline{z}})^{k}(\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{z}})^{k-1}(z-{\overline{z}})^{k},\quad n\geq 1,

(45)

\displaystyle d_{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{k}\frac{(n+k)!}{(n-k)!k!}\frac{1}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{k}}\partial_{z}^{n-k}.

The complex conjugate of
d_{n}
is
{\overline{d}}_{n}
. Because (anti-)differentiation lowers the degree in
z-{\overline{z}}
by one, we have for any
\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}

(46)

D_{n}:(z-{\overline{z}})^{\ell}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}\rightarrow(z-{\overline{z}})^{\ell+2}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}},\quad d_{n}\,({\overline{d}}_{n}):(z-{\overline{z}})^{\ell}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}\rightarrow(z-{\overline{z}})^{\ell-n}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}.

With these definitions we obtain the following theorems.

Theorem 33
.

The kernel of
\Delta_{n}
on
{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}
is
d_{n}h(z)+{\overline{d}}_{n}{\overline{h}}({\overline{z}})
for arbitrary (anti-)holomorphic functions
h(z)
and
{\overline{h}}({\overline{z}})
.

In the above theorem
{\overline{h}}
is not (necessarily) the complex conjugate of
h
. The proof of Theorem
33
is in Section
22
.

Theorem 34
.

Let
n=0,1,2,\ldots
and

(47)

\mathcal{I}_{n}:(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}\rightarrow(z-{\overline{z}})^{-n}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}},\quad\mathcal{I}_{n}f(z)=I^{-}_{n}\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\partial_{z}I^{+}_{n}f(z)\mathrm{d}{\overline{z}}.

Assume
f\in(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}
such that
\mathcal{I}_{n}f
exists. For any
F\in(z-{\overline{z}})^{-n}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}
with
\Delta_{n}F(z)=f(z)

there exist (anti-)meromorphic functions
\phi\in{\mathbb{C}}[z,z^{-1},(z-1)^{-1}]
,
\overline{\phi}\in{\mathbb{C}}[{\overline{z}},{\overline{z}}^{-1},({\overline{z}}-1)^{-1}]
and polynomials
p_{0}
,
p_{1}
of degrees
\leq 2n
such that

(48)

F(z)=[\mathcal{I}_{n}+D_{n}(1-\Delta_{n}\mathcal{I}_{n})]f(z)+d_{n}h(z)+{\overline{d}}_{n}{\overline{h}}({\overline{z}}),

where

(49)

h(z)=\phi(z)+\sum_{s=0,1}p_{s}(z)\log(z-s),\quad{\overline{h}}({\overline{z}})=\overline{\phi}({\overline{z}})+(-1)^{n}\sum_{s=0,1}p_{s}({\overline{z}})\log({\overline{z}}-{\overline{s}}).

The proof of Theorem
34
is in Section
23
.

Example 35
.

For
n=0
the differential operator
\Delta_{0}=\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{z}}
factors.
We get
I_{0}^{\pm}=\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}z
,
D_{0}=0
,
d_{0}={\overline{d}}_{0}=1
, and
\mathcal{I}_{0}=\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}z\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}{\overline{z}}
. Equations (
48
) and (
49
) become

F(z)=\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\int_{\mathrm{sv}}f(z)\mathrm{d}{\overline{z}}\mathrm{d}z+\phi(z)+\overline{\phi}({\overline{z}})+p_{0}\log(z{\overline{z}})+p_{1}\log[(z-1)({\overline{z}}-1)]

with constants
p_{0}
,
p_{1}
. We see that
F(z)
is single-valued and that the branch of the logarithms in (
49
) is insignificant.
The structure of
\phi,\overline{\phi},p_{0},p_{1}
is determined by the condition
F\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}
.

Theorem 36

(The case
n=0
is Lemma 4.5 in
[
35
]
)
.

Let
f_{G}(z)
be a graphical function in even dimensions
\geq 4
.
Then there exists exactly one function
f_{G_{1}}(z)
in
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}
with (G3) of Theorem
5
such that (
43
) holds.

The proof of uniqueness is in Section
24
. If the general solution (
48
) can be calculated, then the above uniqueness result provides
an algorithm to calculate
f_{G_{1}}(z)
from
f_{G}(z)
. This algorithm is detailed in Section
25
.

6.2.
Representation theory and hyperbolic space

We define the differential operators

L_{k}=z^{k}\partial_{z}+{\overline{z}}^{k}\partial_{\overline{z}}.

With these operators we define an
\mathfrak{sl}(2,{\mathbb{C}})
Lie algebra representation on
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}
by

(50)

X=-L_{2},\quad Y=L_{0},\quad H=2L_{1}

with commutation relations
[H,X]=2X
,
[H,Y]=-2Y
,
[X,Y]=H
. The Casimir operator of this representation is

C=(H-1)^{2}+4XY=-4(z-{\overline{z}})^{2}\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{z}}+1

so that the effective Laplace operator (
10
) in
D=2\lambda+2
dimensions becomes

\Delta_{\lambda-1}=-\frac{C-(D-3)^{2}}{4(z-{\overline{z}})^{2}}

Homogeneous solutions of (
9
) for fixed
D
are (sub-)representations of
\mathfrak{sl}(2,{\mathbb{C}})
on
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}
. It is possible that the connection to representation theory
underlies the existence of an explicit solution in Theorem
34
.

Remark 37
.

In physics it is more common to use the operators
L_{+}=X
,
L_{-}=Y
,
L_{z}=H/2
with commutation relations
[L_{z},L_{\pm}]=\pm L_{\pm}
,
[L_{+},L_{-}]=2L_{z}
and Casimir operator

C_{\mathrm{ph}}=L_{z}^{2}-L_{z}+L_{+}L_{-}=-(z-{\overline{z}})^{2}\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{z}}
. In this notation we get
\Delta_{\lambda-1}=-[C_{\mathrm{ph}}-\lambda(\lambda-1)]/(z-{\overline{z}})^{2}
.

In Cartesian coordinates
z=x+\mathrm{i}y
we get
C_{\mathrm{ph}}=(C-1)/4=y^{2}(\partial_{x}^{2}+\partial_{y}^{2})
which is the Laplacian of the hyperbolic space with constant
curvature
-1
in the Poincaré model on the half plane
H=\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Im}z>0\}
. Because of reflection symmetry (Theorem
5
(G1)) we can restrict graphical functions
to
H
without loss of information. So, graphical functions can be viewed as functions on the Poincaré half plane where (
9
) is related to a Klein-Gordon equation
in two-dimensional hyperbolic space with mass-square
\lambda(\lambda-1)
. Single-valuedness of graphical functions, however, is obscured in this picture.

6.3.
Weight
0<\nu_{e}<1

A chain of
\lambda+1-k
edges of weight 1 is (up to a factor) equivalent to an edge of weight
\nu_{e}=k/\lambda
(for
k=1,2,\ldots,\lambda
, see (
53
)).
Iterated use of the algorithm in the previous subsection hence allows one to append edges of weights
0<\nu_{e}<1
,
\nu_{e}\in\frac{1}{\lambda}{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Lemma 38

(see ‘Rule 2’ in
[
24
]
)
.

An internal two-valent vertex can be eliminated by a convolution product:
For
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
and
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}
we have diagrammatically,

(51)

{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

\displaystyle\text{where }\quad c^{(\lambda)}_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}=\frac{\Gamma(\lambda(1-\nu_{1})+1)\Gamma(\lambda(1-\nu_{2})+1)\Gamma(\lambda(\nu_{1}+\nu_{2}-1)-1)}{\Gamma(\lambda\nu_{1})\Gamma(\lambda\nu_{2})\Gamma(\lambda(2-\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})+2)},

provided that the integral on the left hand side of (
51
) converges.

Proof.

An elementary calculation using spherical coordinates gives the following Fourier transform

(52)

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{D}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}x\cdot p}}{\|x\|^{\alpha}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{D}x}{\pi^{D/2}}=\frac{2^{D-\alpha}}{\|p\|^{D-\alpha}}\frac{\Gamma((D-\alpha)/2)}{\Gamma(\alpha/2)}\quad\text{for }0<\alpha<D.

The lemma follows from a commutative diagram that translates the convolution product into a pointwise product after Fourier transformation.
∎

Proposition 39
.

For
k=1,2,\ldots,\lambda
we have

(53)

\frac{k}{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\quad\frac{\displaystyle(\lambda-1)!^{\lambda+1-k}(\lambda-k)!}{\displaystyle(k-1)!}\quad\underbrace{\hbox to225.69pt{\vbox to21.14pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.14459pt\lower-7.83301pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}_{\lambda+1-k}.

Proof.

The proof is by induction from
k=\lambda
down to
k=1
. The case
k=\lambda
is trivial.
Fix
k
in
1,\ldots,\lambda-1
. By induction an edge of weight
\nu_{1}=(k+1)/\lambda
is
(\lambda-1)!^{\lambda-k}(\lambda-k-1)!/k!
times a sequence of
\lambda-k
edges.
We attach an edge of weight
\nu_{2}=1
which, by Lemma
38
, lowers
\nu_{1}
by
1/\lambda
and divides by
(\lambda-1)!(\lambda\nu_{1}-1)(\lambda-\lambda\nu_{1}+1)
.
We obtain the result from

\displaystyle\frac{(\lambda-1)!^{\lambda-k}(\lambda-k-1)!}{k!}(\lambda-1)![(k+1)-1][\lambda-(k+1)+1]=\frac{(\lambda-1)!^{\lambda+1-k}(\lambda-k)!}{(k-1)!}.\qed

Upon completion (Section
2.3
) the above lemma becomes a special case of the factor identity in Section
5.3
.

6.4.
Weight
\nu_{e}<0

If we append an edge of negative weight
\nu_{e}<0
, the graphical function
f_{G_{1}}(z)
in (
6
) is a polynomial in
z
and
{\overline{z}}
.

The vector
z_{2}=(\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Re}z,\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Im}z,0,\ldots,0)^{T}
in (
12
) is attached to an internal vertex
x
. If
\nu_{e}=-k/\lambda
for
k=1,2,\ldots
, then the edge
xz_{2}
contributes to the integrand with the numerator

(54)

\|x-z_{2}\|^{2k}=(\|x\|^{2}-2x\cdot z_{2}+\|z_{2}\|^{2})^{k}=\sum_{k_{0}+k_{1}+k_{2}=k}\frac{k!}{k_{0}!k_{1}!k_{2}!}\|x\|^{2k_{0}}(-2x\cdot z_{2})^{k_{1}}\|z_{2}\|^{2k_{2}},

where
x\cdot z_{2}
is the Euclidean scalar product between
x
and
z_{2}
. The sum on the right hand side is over all partitions of
k
into three non-negative integers.

Lemma 40
.

Let
z_{1},z_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
with
\|z_{1}\|=1
and
A_{G}(0,z_{1},x)
be the Feynman integral (
3
) with external vectors
0,z_{1},x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
.
Then

(55)

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{D}}(x\cdot z_{2})^{k}A_{G}(0,z_{1},x)\frac{\mathrm{d}^{D}x}{\pi^{D/2}}=(z_{1}\cdot z_{2})^{k}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{D}}(x\cdot z_{1})^{k}A_{G}(0,z_{1},x)\frac{\mathrm{d}^{D}x}{\pi^{D/2}}.

Proof.

We expand the scalar product

(x\cdot z_{2})^{k}=\sum_{\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{k}}\Big(\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}z_{2}^{\mu_{\ell}}\Big)\Big(\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}x^{\mu_{\ell}}\Big).

The first factor is constant and can be extracted from the integral. The second factor gives a tensor of rank
k
. The remaining integral solely depends on the unit vector
z_{1}
.
Hence

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{D}}\Big(\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}x^{\mu_{\ell}}\Big)A_{G}(0,z_{1},x)\frac{\mathrm{d}^{D}x}{\pi^{D/2}}=P\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}z_{1}^{\mu_{\ell}}

for some constant
P\in{\mathbb{R}}
. We multiply both sides with
\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}z_{1}^{\mu_{\ell}}
and sum over the indices
\mu_{\ell}
to obtain

P=\sum_{\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{k}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{D}}\Big(\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}x^{\mu_{\ell}}z_{1}^{\mu_{\ell}}\Big)A_{G}(0,z_{1},x)\frac{\mathrm{d}^{D}x}{\pi^{D/2}}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{D}}(x\cdot z_{1})^{k}A_{G}(0,z_{1},x)\frac{\mathrm{d}^{D}x}{\pi^{D/2}}.

This gives the desired result.
∎

The integral on the right hand side of (
55
) is a real number which can be expressed in terms of periods (see Section
3
) by the identity

(56)

x\cdot z_{1}=\frac{1+\|x\|^{2}-\|x-z_{1}\|^{2}}{2}.

Theorem 41
.

Consider a graphical function
f_{G_{1}(k)}(z)
whose external vertex
z
is attached to a single internal vertex
x
by an edge of weight
\nu_{xz}=-k/\lambda
,
k=1,2,\ldots
(see picture below).
Let
P_{G(k_{0},k_{1})}
be the period (see Section
3
) of the graph
G(k_{0},k_{1})=G_{1}(k)\backslash\{xz\}\cup\{x0,x1\}
where the edges
x0
and
x1

have weights
\nu_{x0}=-k_{0}/\lambda
and
\nu_{x1}=-k_{1}/\lambda
. Then
P_{G(k_{0},k_{1})}
exists for
k_{0},k_{1}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
,
k_{0}+k_{1}\leq k
and

(57)

\displaystyle f_{G_{1}(k)}(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k_0+k_1+k_2=k}{0\leq k_0,k_1,k_2}}p^{k}_{k_{0},k_{1},k_{2}}(z,{\overline{z}})~P_{G(k_{0},k_{1})},

with polynomials

\displaystyle p^{k}_{k_{0},k_{1},k_{2}}(z,{\overline{z}})

\displaystyle=\frac{k!(2-z-{\overline{z}})^{k_{0}}(z+{\overline{z}})^{k_{1}}(2z{\overline{z}}-z-{\overline{z}})^{k_{2}}}{2^{k}k_{0}!k_{1}!k_{2}!}.

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{25.8073pt}{-30.66866pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{}{}{}
