By rotational invariance the cases
{|{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{ext}}}|}=0
and
{|{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{ext}}}|}=1
are identical, see Section
18
.
If
{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{ext}}}=\{z_{1},z_{2}\}
, then
f_{G}^{\angle}(z_{1},z_{2})
is a polynomial in
z_{1}\cdot z_{2}
.

(3)

It is possible to restrict the expansion (
104
) to simple graphs with weights
\nu_{e}(g)=1
(or any other weight). In this case the coefficients
c_{g}
are
well defined rational functions in the weights
\nu_{e}(G)
, see Example
74
.

(4)

It is possible to derive constraints on the weights
n_{e}
for non-vanishing angular graphical functions.
For example, one may consider an internal edge cut
\mathcal{C}
(i.e. one of the cut subgraphs has only internal vertices).
Then
f_{G}^{\angle}=0
unless for every edge
e\in\mathcal{C}

n_{e}\leq_{2}\sum_{e\neq f\in\mathcal{C}}n_{f}.

We do not need this generalized triangle identity here and therefore leave it unproved.

18.
Angular periods

If the graph
G
has no external vertices,
\mathcal{V}_{G}={\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}
, (or
G
has one external vertex), its angular graphical function is a constant angular period,

(105)

P^{\angle}_{G}=f^{\angle}_{G}(\emptyset).

By the previous section it is clear that for fixed
n_{e}
,
e\in\mathcal{E}
, angular periods in dimension
D=2\lambda+2\geq 3
are rational functions in
\lambda
and
\nu_{e}
.

Example 79
.

Consider the tetrahedron with vertices 1, 2, 3, 4. For tuples of integer edge-weights
(n_{12},n_{13},n_{14},n_{34},n_{24},n_{23})
we get the angular periods (compare Example
74
)

\displaystyle\displaystyle(0,0,0,0,0,0)

\displaystyle:1,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\!(2,0,0,0,0,0)

\displaystyle:-\frac{\lambda^{2}\nu_{12}(1-\nu_{12})}{\lambda+1},

\displaystyle\displaystyle(4,0,0,0,0,0)

\displaystyle:\frac{\lambda^{2}\nu_{12}(\lambda\nu_{12}+1)(1-\nu_{12})(\lambda(1-\nu_{12})-1)}{2(\lambda+1)(\lambda+2)},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\!(2,2,0,0,0,0)

\displaystyle:\frac{\lambda^{2}\nu_{12}\nu_{13}(1-\nu_{12})(1-\nu_{13})}{(\lambda+1)^{2}},

\displaystyle\displaystyle(1,1,0,0,0,1)

\displaystyle:\frac{2\lambda^{3}\nu_{12}\nu_{13}\nu_{23}}{(\lambda+1)^{2}},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\!(1,1,0,1,1,0)

\displaystyle:\frac{2\lambda^{4}\nu_{12}\nu_{13}\nu_{34}\nu_{24}}{(\lambda+1)^{3}}.

Up to permutations these are the only non-zero tetrahedra periods with
\sum_{e}n_{e}\leq 4
.

In the above example we see that for general weights
\nu_{e}
angular periods can be negative. If
D=4
and
\nu_{e}=1
for all
e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}
, this is not the case.

Theorem 80
.

Let
P_{G}^{\angle}
be the angular period of a graph
G
with edge-weights
\nu_{e}=1
for all
e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}
in
D=4
dimensions. Then
P_{G}^{\angle}\geq 0
.

Proof.

We use that a unit vector
x
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
can be identified with an
SU(2)
group element via

x\sim\Big(\begin{array}[]{cc}x_{1}&x_{2}\\
-{\overline{x}}_{2}&{\overline{x}}_{1}\end{array}\Big),\quad\text{where }x_{1},x_{2}\in{\mathbb{C}}\quad\text{and}\quad x=(\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Re}x_{1},\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Im}x_{1},\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Re}x_{2},\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Im}x_{2})^{T}.

We obtain the relation

\mathrm{Tr}\,xy^{-1}=2\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Re}\,(x_{1}\overline{y_{1}}+x_{2}\overline{y_{2}})=2\cos\phi_{xy}=C^{1}_{1}(\cos\phi_{xy}),

where
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
are the complex coordinates of
y
. The matrix of
x
is the two-dimensional representation
R^{(2)}(x)
of the
SU(2)
element
x
with character

\mathrm{Tr}\,R^{(2)}(x)=\chi_{2}(x)
. The group
SU(2)
has one irreducible representation
R^{(n+1)}
in each finite dimension
n+1\geq 1
. Tensor products of irreducible
SU(2)
representations
reduce to irreducible representations according to

R^{(n_{1}+1)}(x)\otimes R^{(n_{2}+1)}(x)=\sum_{|n_{1}-n_{2}|\leq_{2}n_{3}\leq_{2}n_{1}+n_{2}}SR^{(n_{3}+1)}(x)S^{-1}

for some invertible matrix
S
. By unitarity of the representations we can choose
S
to be unitary,
S^{-1}=S^{\dagger}
(in fact one can choose
S
to be orthogonal). Using indices this gives

(106)

R^{(n_{1}+1)}_{m_{1},m_{1}^{\prime}}(x)R^{(n_{2}+1)}_{m_{2},m_{2}^{\prime}}(x)=\sum_{|n_{1}-n_{2}|\leq_{2}n_{3}\leq_{2}n_{1}+n_{2}}\;\sum_{m_{3},m_{3}^{\prime}=1}^{n_{3}+1}S^{n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}}_{m_{1},m_{2},m_{3}}R^{(n_{3}+1)}_{m_{3},m_{3}^{\prime}}(x)\overline{S}^{n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}}_{m_{1}^{\prime},m_{2}^{\prime},m_{3}^{\prime}},

where we made the dependence of
S
on
n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}
explicit. The matrix index of
S
is the bi-label
m_{1},m_{2}
and the label
m_{3}
.
The
S^{n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}}_{m_{1},m_{2},m_{3}}
are related to the 3-
j
symbols in quantum mechanics (see e.g.
[
32
]
and the references therein).
By taking traces on both sides we obtain the addition formula for the
SU(2)
characters

\chi_{n_{1}+1}(x)\chi_{n_{2}+1}(x)=\sum_{|n_{1}-n_{2}|\leq_{2}n_{3}\leq_{2}n_{1}+n_{2}}\chi_{n_{3}+1}(x).

This formula mirrors the addition formula for the Gegenbauer polynomials
C^{1}_{n}
,

C^{1}_{n_{1}}(x)C^{1}_{n_{2}}(x)=\sum_{|n_{1}-n_{2}|\leq_{2}n_{3}\leq_{2}n_{1}+n_{2}}C^{1}_{n_{3}}(x),

which follows from (
93
). Moreover, we have
\chi_{1}(xy^{-1})=C^{1}_{0}(\cos\phi_{xy})=1
. With the initial conditions for the first two cases
the addition formulae fully determine the characters and Gegenbauer polynomials for higher indices. We get

C^{1}_{n}(\cos\phi_{xy})=\chi_{n+1}(xy^{-1})=\sum_{m,m^{\prime}=1}^{n+1}R^{(n+1)}_{m,m^{\prime}}(x)\overline{R}^{(n+1)}_{m,m^{\prime}}(y).

The left hand side is the angular propagator (
97
) for weights
(\nu_{e},n_{e})=(1,n)
. To handle the formal asymmetry in
x
and
y
we orient the edges of the angular graph

G
by the convention that
ij
is the edge from
i
to
j
. From the definition (
98
), (
105
) of the angular period we get

P_{G}^{\angle}=\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{V}_{G}|}\int_{S_{D-1}}\Omega^{x_{i}}_{D-1}\Big)\prod_{e=ij\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}\sum_{m,m^{\prime}=1}^{n_{e}+1}R^{(n_{e}+1)}_{m,m^{\prime}}(x_{i})\overline{R}^{(n_{e}+1)}_{m,m^{\prime}}(x_{j}).

We interchange the finite sums with the integral and obtain

(107)

P_{G}^{\angle}=\sum_{m_{1},m_{1}^{\prime}=1}^{n_{1}+1}\cdots\sum_{m_{|\mathcal{E}_{G}|},m_{|\mathcal{E}_{G}|}^{\prime}=1}^{n_{|\mathcal{E}_{G}|}+1}\prod_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{V}_{G}|}\int_{S_{D-1}}\Omega^{x_{i}}_{D-1}\Big(\prod_{j:ij\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}R^{(n_{ij}+1)}_{m_{ij},m_{ij}^{\prime}}(x_{i})\Big)\Big(\prod_{j:ji\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}\overline{R}^{(n_{ji}+1)}_{m_{ji},m_{ji}^{\prime}}(x_{i})\Big),

where the empty product is 1. Note that we label the edges by
1,\ldots,|\mathcal{E}_{G}|
and also by the directed pairs of their adjacent vertices.
Iterated use of (
106
) gives

\prod_{j:ij\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}R^{(n_{ij}+1)}_{m_{ij},m_{ij}^{\prime}}(x_{i})=\sum_{M,M^{\prime},N}S^{(n_{ij})_{j},N}_{(m_{ij})_{j},M}R^{(N+1)}_{M,M^{\prime}}(x_{i})\overline{S}^{(n_{ij})_{j},N}_{(m_{ij}^{\prime})_{j},M^{\prime}},

where we have defined multi-
j
-type symbols
S
, see
[
32
]
.
If on the left hand side the product over
j
is empty, the right hand side is defined as
1=R^{(1)}_{1,1}
whereas in the case of a single factor the formula is trivial.
Otherwise we explicitly have

S^{(n_{ij})_{j},N}_{(m_{ij})_{j},M}=\sum_{M_{1},\ldots,M_{k}}\sum_{N_{1},\ldots,N_{k}}S^{n_{1},n_{2},N_{2}}_{m_{1},m_{2},M_{2}}S^{N_{2},n_{3},N_{3}}_{M_{2},m_{3},M_{3}}\cdots S^{N_{k-1},n_{k},N}_{M_{k-1},m_{k},M},

where we assumed that the edges in the product are labeled from 1 to
k
.

With the orthogonality relation of representations (see e.g.
[
40
]
)

\int_{S_{D-1}}\Omega^{x}_{D-1}R^{(n_{1}+1)}_{m_{1},m_{1}^{\prime}}(x)\overline{R}^{(n_{2}+1)}_{m_{2},m_{2}^{\prime}}(x)=\frac{\delta_{n_{1},n_{2}}\delta_{m_{1},m_{2}}\delta_{m_{1}^{\prime},m_{2}^{\prime}}}{n_{1}+1}

the integral over
x_{i}
in (
107
) gives

\sum_{M,M^{\prime},N}\frac{S^{(n_{ij})_{j},N}_{(m_{ij})_{j},M}\overline{S}^{(n_{ij})_{j},N}_{(m_{ij}^{\prime})_{j},M^{\prime}}\overline{S}^{(n_{ji})_{j},N}_{(m_{ji})_{j},M}S^{(n_{ji})_{j},N}_{(m_{ji}^{\prime})_{j},M^{\prime}}}{N+1}.

With this result the terms with unprimed and primed indices factor in (
107
),

P_{G}^{\angle}=\frac{1}{\prod_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{V}_{G}|}(N_{i}+1)}\sum_{N_{1},\ldots,N_{|\mathcal{V}_{G}|}}\Big|\sum_{m_{1},\ldots,m_{|\mathcal{E}_{G}|}}\sum_{M_{1},\ldots,M_{|\mathcal{V}_{G}|}}\prod_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{V}_{G}|}S^{(n_{ij})_{j},N_{i}}_{(m_{ij})_{j},M_{i}}\overline{S}^{(n_{ji})_{j},N_{i}}_{(m_{ji})_{j},M_{i}}\Big|^{2}.

This expression for
P_{G}^{\angle}
is explicitly non-negative.
∎

Example 81
.

For the
n
-cycle
C_{n}
with only internal vertices (and edge-weights
(1,n_{i,i+1})
) we get the angular period from integrating (
72
) in Example
72
over
z_{2}
.
By orthogonality (
95
) of the Gegenbauer polynomials we get

(108)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} P_{C_{n}}^{\angle}&=\Big(\frac{\lambda}{\lambda+n_{z_{1},2}}\Big)^{n-1}\!\Big(\prod_{i=2}^{n}\delta_{n_{i-1,i},n_{i,i+1}}\Big)C^{\lambda}_{n_{z_{1},2}}(1)=\\
&=\genfrac{(}{)}{0.0pt}{}{n_{z_1,2}+2\lambda-1}{n_{z_1,2}}\Big(\frac{\lambda}{\lambda+n_{z_{1},2}}\Big)^{n-1}\!\Big(\prod_{i=2}^{n}\delta_{n_{i-1,i},n_{i,i+1}}\Big),\end{aligned}

where we have used (
94
).

Example 82
.

Consider the tetrahedron with vertices
1,2,3,4
and edge-weights
\nu_{e}=1
for all edges
e
.
For integer edge-weights
(n_{12},n_{13},n_{14},n_{34},n_{24},n_{23})
we get the angular periods (compare Example
79
)

(0,0,0,0,0,0):1,\quad(1,1,0,0,0,1):\frac{2\lambda^{3}}{(\lambda+1)^{2}},\quad(1,1,0,1,1,0):\frac{2\lambda^{4}}{(\lambda+1)^{3}}.

Up to permutations these are the only non-zero tetrahedra periods with
\nu_{e}=1
and
\sum_{e}n_{e}\leq 4
. For
n_{e}=2
we get

(2,2,2,2,2,2):\frac{8\lambda^{7}(2\lambda+1)(\lambda^{2}+4\lambda-3)}{(\lambda+2)^{3}(\lambda+3)^{3}}

which is
-\frac{6}{42875}
in three dimensions. For
D\geq 4
the angular period of the tetrahedron with
n_{e}=2
is positive.

The proof of Theorem
80
explicitly uses the group structure of the unit-sphere in four dimensions.
It does not generalize to higher dimensions. Still, angular periods may be positive for
D>4
.

Question 83
.

Does Theorem
80
hold for (some) dimensions
D>4
?

Example
82
admits the possibility that Theorem
80
holds for all
D\geq 4
and, in particular, for even
D\geq 4
. So, non-negativity of angular periods may still be
a viable path to extend the validity of (G3) in Theorem
5
. Note that Theorem
5
has been excessively tested by the calculation of many Feynman periods with graphical functions.

19.
Gegenbauer expansion

We want to prove that it is possible to express graphical functions as multiple sums over radial and angular graphical functions
[
17
]
.
The obstacle is to legitimate interchanging the Gegenbauer sum with the position space integrals. We expect that this is always possible but
we only prove a special case with a positivity condition on angular periods, see Theorem
80
and Question
83
.

Theorem 84
.

Let
G
be a graph such that the graphical function
f_{G}(z)
exists in
D=2\lambda+2\geq 3
(odd or even) dimensions.
Let
G(\mathbf{n})
be the graph
G
with additional non-negative integer edge-weights
\mathbf{n}=n_{1},\ldots,n_{|\mathcal{E}_{G}|}

which vanish for every edge that is incident to the external vertex 0. Moreover, for
n_{1z}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
we define
G(\mathbf{n},n_{1z})
as the graph that is obtained
from
G(\mathbf{n})\backslash\{0\}
by adding an edge
1z
with weights
\nu_{1z}=1
and
n_{1z}
. We assume that the angular period (
105
) fulfills

(109)

P_{G(\mathbf{n},n_{1z})}^{\angle}\geq 0\quad\text{for all }\mathbf{n},n_{1z}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}.

Then the graphical function
f_{G}(z)
admits an absolutely convergent Gegenbauer expansion for
z=Z\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\phi}
,
Z\neq 1
,

(110)

f_{G}(z)=\Big(\frac{2}{\Gamma(\lambda+1)}\Big)^{|{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}|}\sum_{\mathbf{n}}f_{G(\mathbf{n})}^{R}(Z)f_{G(\mathbf{n})\backslash\{0\}}^{\angle}(\cos\phi).

The radial and angular graphical functions
f_{G(\mathbf{n})}^{R}
and
f_{G(\mathbf{n})\backslash\{0\}}^{\angle}
are defined in Sections
16
and
17
.

Proof.

We use spherical coordinates (
81
). From (
11
) we get
z_{1}=(1,0,\ldots,0)^{T}
and
z_{2}=(Z\cos\phi,

Z\sin\phi,

0,\ldots,0)^{T}
.
Propagators in (
3
) which are not attached to 0 expand into Gegenbauer polynomials, see (
92
),

(111)

\frac{1}{\|x-y\|^{2\lambda\nu_{xy}}}=\frac{1}{(XY)^{\lambda\nu_{xy}}}\sum_{n_{xy}=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{X}{Y}\right)_{<}^{n_{xy}+\lambda\nu_{xy}}C^{\lambda\nu_{xy}}_{n_{xy}}(\cos\phi_{xy}),

where
\phi_{xy}
is the angle between
x
and
y
and
(x)_{<}=x
if
x<1
and
x^{-1}
otherwise. Propagators of edges which are attached to 0 equal their radial propagators (
82
).

The integration measure splits into radial and angular coordinates according to (see (
91
))

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{D}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{D}x_{i}}{\pi^{D/2}}=\frac{\text{vol}\,S_{D-1}}{\pi^{D/2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}XX^{D-1}\int_{S_{D-1}}\Omega^{x}_{D-1}=\frac{2}{\Gamma(\lambda+1)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}XX^{D-1}\int_{S_{D-1}}\Omega^{x}_{D-1}.

By Fubini we can do all angular integrals first. The domain of integration is compact and the integral is absolutely convergent because by (
94
) we get for
X\neq Y
,

\frac{1}{(XY)^{\lambda\nu_{xy}}}\sum_{n_{xy}=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{X}{Y}\right)_{<}^{n_{xy}+\lambda\nu_{xy}}|C^{\lambda\nu_{xy}}_{n_{xy}}(\cos\phi_{xy})|\leq\frac{1}{|X-Y|^{2\lambda\nu_{xy}}}<\infty.

The angular integration over the Gegenbauer polynomials gives the angular graphical function
f_{G(\mathbf{n})\backslash\{0\}}^{\angle}(\cos\phi)
.
It admits an expansion into Gegenbauer polynomials

(112)

f_{G(\mathbf{n})\backslash\{0\}}^{\angle}(\cos\phi)=\sum_{k\geq 0}f_{G(\mathbf{n})\backslash\{0\},k}^{\angle}C^{\lambda}_{k}(\cos\phi).

If we multiply
f_{G(\mathbf{n})\backslash\{0\}}^{\angle}
with
C^{\lambda}_{n_{1z}}(\cos\phi)
and integrate over
\int_{S_{D-1}}\Omega^{z}_{D-1}
, orthogonality
(
95
) of Gegenbauer polynomials provides an expansion into angular periods,

f_{G(\mathbf{n})\backslash\{0\}}^{\angle}(\cos\phi)=\sum_{n_{1z}\geq 0}\frac{\lambda+n_{1z}}{\lambda C^{\lambda}_{n_{1z}}(1)}P_{G(\mathbf{n},n_{1z})}^{\angle}C^{\lambda}_{n_{1z}}(\cos\phi).

Because
P_{G(\mathbf{n},n_{1z})}^{\angle}\geq 0
we get from (
94
) the estimate

|f_{G(\mathbf{n})\backslash\{0\}}^{\angle}(\cos\phi)|\leq\sum_{n_{1z}\geq 0}\frac{\lambda+n_{1z}}{\lambda}P_{G(\mathbf{n},n_{1z})}^{\angle}=f_{G(\mathbf{n})\backslash\{0\}}^{\angle}(1).

The integral over the radial variables is absolutely convergent,

\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{|{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}|}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}X_{i}X_{i}^{D-1}\Big)\Big|\Big(\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}p^{R}_{e}\Big)f_{G(\mathbf{n})\backslash\{0\}}^{\angle}(\cos\phi)\Big|\leq f_{G}(Z)<\infty

for
1\neq Z>0
. In the above estimate we reversed the interchange of the sum and the angular integral to obtain
f_{G}(Z\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\phi})
for
\phi=0
. Finiteness follows from
(G2) in Theorem
5
.

By absolute convergence we can interchange the sum with the radial integrals which gives (
110
) for
Z\neq 1
.
∎

Corollary 85
.

Let
G
be an uncompleted graph such that the Feynman period
P_{G}
exists in
D=2\lambda+2\geq 3
dimensions (see Section
3
).
Let
G(\mathbf{n})\cup\{Z\}
be the graph of the corresponding constant radial graphical function with isolated external vertex
Z
.
If the angular period
P_{G(\mathbf{n})\backslash\{0\}}^{\angle}\geq 0
for all
\mathbf{n}
, then

(113)

P_{G}=\Big(\frac{2}{\Gamma(\lambda+1)}\Big)^{|\mathcal{V}_{G}|-2}\sum_{\mathbf{n}}f_{G(\mathbf{n})\cup\{Z\}}^{R}P_{G(\mathbf{n})\backslash\{0\}}^{\angle}.

Proof.

Let
G_{0,1,z}
be the constant graphical function which arises from the period
G
by identifying any two vertices with 0 and 1 and adding an isolated vertex
z
.
We add an edge
1z
with weight
(1,n_{1z})
and consider the period
P_{G(\mathbf{n},n_{1z})}^{\angle}
as in Theorem
84
. By orthogonality (
95
) the integration over
the vertex
z
gives zero unless
n_{1z}=0
. In this case the edge
1z
is absent and
P_{G(\mathbf{n},n_{1z})}^{\angle}\geq 0
follows from
P_{G(\mathbf{n})\backslash\{0\}}^{\angle}\geq 0
.
Theorem
84
gives the result.
∎

Conjecture 86
.

The Gegenbauer expansion (
110
) is valid for all graphical functions.

In physics the Gegenbauer
x
-space technique is considered a well established tool
[
17
]
. It tacitly assumes that the Gegenbauer expansion in the integrand interchanges with the

x
-space integrion as in Conjecture
86
.

Example 87
.

Consider the wheel with
n
spokes
W\!S_{n,D}
in Figure
4
and assume that the (odd or even) dimension
D\leq 2n-2
for convergence.
We want to calculate the period
P_{W\!S_{n,D}}
. The hub has label 0 so that
W\!S_{n,D}\backslash\{0\}=C_{n}

whose angular period is calculated in Example
81
. The result (
108
) is non-negative.
The radial period can be derived from Example
70
by integrating (
89
) over
Z
with the measure

Z^{2\lambda+1}\mathrm{d}Z
. Alternatively one can consider the wheel with
n+1
spokes at
Z=1
(the triangle
01Z
contributes with a trivial factor 1 to the radial graphical function for
Z=1
).
We get

f_{W\!S_{n,D}\cup\{Z\}}^{R}=2^{n}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{2\pi}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{n_{i,i+1}+\lambda}{P^{2}+(n_{i,i+1}+\lambda)^{2}}.

With (
108
) all the
n_{i,i+1}
are identified and we obtain

\displaystyle P_{W\!S_{n,D}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{2n-1}}{\Gamma(\lambda+1)^{n-1}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\genfrac{(}{)}{0.0pt}{}{k+2\lambda-1}{k}\Big(\frac{\lambda}{\lambda+k}\Big)^{n-1}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{2\pi}\Big(\frac{k+\lambda}{P^{2}+(k+\lambda)^{2}}\Big)^{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{2n-1}}{\Gamma(\lambda)^{n-1}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{2\pi(P^{2}+1)^{n}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\genfrac{(}{)}{0.0pt}{}{k+2\lambda-1}{k}}{(k+\lambda)^{2n-2}},

where we scaled the integration variable
P
with
k+\lambda
. The residue theorem gives

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{2\pi(P^{2}+1)^{n}}=\mathrm{i}\,\mathrm{res}_{\mathrm{i}}\frac{1}{(P^{2}+1)^{n}}=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{(n-1)!}\frac{\partial}{\partial_{P}^{n-1}}\Big|_{\mathrm{i}}\frac{1}{(P+\mathrm{i})^{n}}\\
=\frac{1}{2^{2n-1}}\genfrac{(}{)}{0.0pt}{}{2n-2}{n-1}.

We obtain the formula

P_{W\!S_{n,D}}=\frac{\genfrac{(}{)}{0.0pt}{}{2n-2}{n-1}}{(2\lambda-1)!\Gamma(\lambda)^{n-1}}\sum_{k=\lambda}^{\infty}\frac{(k+\lambda-1)(k+\lambda-2)\cdots(k-\lambda+2)(k-\lambda+1)}{k^{2n-2}}.

For even
D
we extend the sum to
k=1
(the surplus terms have zero numerator). We find that a factor of
k
cancels and get by pairing the factors in the numerator

(114)

P_{W\!S_{n,D}}=\frac{\genfrac{(}{)}{0.0pt}{}{2n-2}{n-1}}{(2\lambda-1)!(\lambda-1)!^{n-1}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\prod_{\ell=1}^{\lambda-1}(k^{2}-\ell^{2})}{k^{2n-3}},\quad\text{if}\;D\leq 2n-2\;\text{is even}.

For odd
D
the sum over
k
ranges over half-integers from
\lambda
to infinity. We are free to extend the sum to
k=1/2
. If we add and subtract the sum over the integers from
1 to infinity, we get

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}P_{W\!S_{n,D}}=\frac{\genfrac{(}{)}{0.0pt}{}{2n-2}{n-1}}{(2\lambda-1)!(\lambda-1)!^{n-1}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{2^{2n-2\lambda-1}\prod_{\ell=1}^{\lambda-1/2}(k^{2}-(2\ell-1)^{2})-\prod_{\ell=1}^{\lambda-1/2}(k^{2}-\ell^{2})}{k^{2n-2}},\;\\
\text{if}\;D\leq 2n-3\;\text{is odd}.\end{gathered}

The products on the right hand sides expand to
{\mathbb{Q}}
-linear combinations of single zetas of odd weights for even
D
and even weights for odd
D
.

20.
Proof of Theorem
5

In this section we prove (G3) in Theorem
5
. The main task will be to prove the case
s=0
in (
18
) with the identity in (
19
).
We will keep the dimension and the weights general when possible to show that Conjecture
6
follows from Conjecture
86
.

We start proving that the case
s=1
in (
18
) follows from the case
s=0
. We use (
16
) for the graph
G
with external vertices
a,b,c
which
we first interpret as
a=z_{0}
,
b=z_{1}
,
c=z_{2}
yielding the function of the graph
G_{0,1,z}
(indexed by the external labels).
Then we interpret
a,b,c
as
z_{1},z_{0},z_{2}
interchanging the role of
z
and
1-z
in (
4
). We hence obtain the graph
G_{1,0,1-z}
.
From (
16
) we get (alternatively one can use completion, see Sections
2.3
and
2.4
)

\|z_{1}-z_{0}\|^{-2\lambda N_{G}}f_{G_{0,1,z}}=\|z_{0}-z_{1}\|^{-2\lambda N_{G}}f_{G_{1,0,1-z}}.

This implies
f_{G_{1,0,1-z}}=f_{G_{0,1,z}}
and (
18
) follows for
s=1
by swapping
0\leftrightarrow 1
and
z\leftrightarrow 1-z
.

Next we interpret
a,b,c
as
z_{0},z_{2},z_{1}
which is equivalent to swapping
z\leftrightarrow z^{-1}
in (
4
). From (
16
) we get

\|z_{1}-z_{0}\|^{-2\lambda N_{G}}f_{G_{0,1,z}}=\|z_{2}-z_{0}\|^{-2\lambda N_{G}}f_{G_{0,z^{-1},1}}.

With (
4
) this implies
f_{G_{0,z^{-1},1}}=(z{\overline{z}})^{\lambda N_{G}}f_{G_{0,1,z}}
(see (
85
)).
We expand
f_{G_{0,1,z}}
at
s=0
and substitute
z\mapsto z^{-1}
to get for
|z|>1
,

f_{G_{0,z,1}}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{|{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}|}\sum_{m,{\overline{m}}=M_{0}(G_{0,1,z})}^{\infty}(-1)^{\ell}c_{\ell,m,{\overline{m}}}^{0}(G_{0,1,z})(\log z{\overline{z}})^{\ell}z^{-m-\lambda N_{G}}{\overline{z}}^{-{\overline{m}}-\lambda N_{G}}.

With the new summation indices
m^{\prime}=-m-\lambda N_{G}
,
{\overline{m}}^{\prime}=-{\overline{m}}-\lambda N_{G}
we get (
20
) where

M_{\infty}(G_{0,z,1})=-M_{0}(G_{0,1,z})-\lambda N_{G}.

This gives the identity in (
21
) from (
19
) for
s=0
by interchanging 1 and
z
.

We now prove the inequality in (
19
). For
s=0,1
and any
\emptyset\neq\mathcal{V}\subseteq{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}
let
G_{s}=G[\mathcal{V}\cup\{s,z\}]
.
Let
\nu_{z}(G_{s})
be the sum of weights adjacent to
z
in
G_{s}
. Because
G_{s}
is a subgraph of
G
we have
\nu_{z}(G_{s})\leq\nu_{z}^{>}
.
We consider the graph
G[\mathcal{V}\cup\{s\}]=G_{s}\backslash\{z\}
. Note that
G[\mathcal{V}\cup\{s\}]
has at least one internal vertex.
By convergence (
26
) we get
N_{G[\mathcal{V}\cup\{s\}]}<0
. Integrality of the weights (
\lambda\nu_{e}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
) implies
\lambda N_{G[\mathcal{V}\cup\{s\}]}\leq-1
.
We multiply

\sum_{e\in G_{s}}\nu_{e}=\sum_{e\in G[\mathcal{V}\cup\{s\}]}\nu_{e}+\nu_{z}(G_{s})\leq\sum_{e\in G[\mathcal{V}\cup\{s\}]}\nu_{e}+\nu_{z}^{>}

with
\lambda
and subtract
(\lambda+1)|\mathcal{V}|
to obtain

\lambda N_{G_{s}}\leq\lambda N_{G[\mathcal{V}\cup\{s\}]}+\lambda\nu_{z}^{>}\leq-1+\lambda\nu_{z}^{>}.

This implies
-\max_{\emptyset\neq\mathcal{V}\subseteq{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}}\lambda N_{G_{s}}\geq 1-\lambda\nu_{z}^{>}
. For
\mathcal{V}=\emptyset
we get
G_{s}=sz
and the inequality in (
19
) follows.

To prove the inequality in (
21
) we choose any proper subset
\mathcal{V}
of
{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}
. Depending on the number of vertices in
{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}\backslash\mathcal{V}

we split the edges of
G\backslash G[\mathcal{V}\cup\{0,1\}]
into the three sets
\mathcal{E}_{2}=G[{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}\backslash\mathcal{V}]
,
\mathcal{E}_{1}=\{uv,\,u\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}\backslash\mathcal{V},\,v\in\mathcal{V}\cup\{0,1,z\}\}
,
and
\mathcal{E}_{0}=\{vz,\,v\in\mathcal{V}\cup\{0,1\}\}
. We get

(117)

\lambda N_{G}-\lambda N_{G[\mathcal{V}\cup\{0,1\}]}+(\lambda+1)({|{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}|}-|\mathcal{V}|)=\sum_{e\in G\backslash G[\mathcal{V}\cup\{0,1\}]}\lambda\nu_{e}\geq\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{2}}\lambda\nu_{e}+\lambda\nu_{z}^{<}.

We consider the graph
{\overline{G}}[{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}\cup\{\infty\}\backslash\mathcal{V}]
which has at least one internal vertex. Convergence (
26
) gives

\sum_{e\in{\overline{G}}[{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}\cup\{\infty\}\backslash\mathcal{V}]}\nu_{e}-\frac{\lambda+1}{\lambda}({|{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}|}-|\mathcal{V}|)<0.

Edges in
{\overline{G}}[{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}\cup\{\infty\}\backslash\mathcal{V}]
are either in
\mathcal{E}_{2}
or they have one vertex
\infty
. By completion, see Definition
8
, we have

\lambda\nu_{v\infty}=D-\lambda\nu_{v}
where
\nu_{v}
is the sum of all weights adjacent in
G
to the vertex
v\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}\backslash\mathcal{V}
. Altogether, convergence implies

\sum_{v\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}\backslash\mathcal{V}}(2\lambda+2-\lambda\nu_{v})+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{2}}\lambda\nu_{e}-(\lambda+1)({|{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}|}-|\mathcal{V}|)<0.

Because
\sum_{v\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}\backslash\mathcal{V}}\nu_{v}=2\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{2}}\nu_{e}+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{1}}\nu_{e}
we have

(\lambda+1)({|{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}|}-|\mathcal{V}|)-\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{2}}\lambda\nu_{e}<0.

With integrality of the weights and (
117
) we obtain

-\lambda N_{G}+\lambda N_{G[\mathcal{V}\cup\{0,1\}]}\leq-1-\lambda\nu_{z}^{<}.

For
\mathcal{V}={\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}
we get
-\lambda N_{G}+\lambda N_{G[\mathcal{V}\cup\{0,1\}]}=-\lambda\nu_{z}
and the inequality in (
21
) follows.

We are left with the task to prove (G3) for
s=0
. We assume
Z<1
and use the Gegenbauer expansion into radial and angular graphical functions in Theorem
84
.
Here, we have to specialize to
D=4
and
\nu_{e}=1
in (
17
) because we need Theorem
80
to establish condition (
109
).

In (
88
) we have
\log Z=\log(z{\overline{z}})/2
. We split the power of
Z
into two parts: One part is

Z^{-2\lambda N_{G[\mathcal{V}\cup\{0,z\}]}}=(z{\overline{z}})^{-\lambda N_{G[\mathcal{V}\cup\{0,z\}]}}=(z{\overline{z}})^{M}\quad\text{with }M\geq M_{0},

where
M_{0}
is defined in (
19
). Because
\lambda\nu_{e}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
for all edges
e
we get
M\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

We combine the second part with the angular graphical function in (
104
) which specializes to

f^{\angle}_{G}(1,z)=\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}}c_{g}f^{\angle}_{g}(1,z).

The vertices of the external graph
g
are a subset of
\{1,z\}
.
If
g
has the vertices 1 and
z
, then
f_{g}^{\angle}(1,z)
is a polynomial in
\cos(\phi)
of degree
\sum_{e}n_{e}(g)
which is symmetric if
\sum_{e}n_{e}(g)
is even and anti-symmetric otherwise.
We have
Z\cos(\phi)=(z+{\overline{z}})/2
and
Z^{2}=z{\overline{z}}
. From (
103
) for
\mathcal{V}\cup\{0,z\}\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{G}
we get with the second part of the power of
Z
in (
88
),

Z^{\sum_{e\in\mathcal{C}^{G}_{\mathcal{V}\cup\{0,z\}}}n_{e}}f^{\angle}_{g}(1,z)=P(z,{\overline{z}}),\quad\text{with }P\in{\mathbb{R}}[z,{\overline{z}}].

If
g
has one or zero vertices, then
f_{g}^{\angle}(1,z)
is constant and
\sum_{e\in\mathcal{C}^{G}_{\mathcal{V}\cup\{0,z\}}}n_{e}(G)
is even.
In this case
P\in{\mathbb{R}}[z{\overline{z}}]\subset{\mathbb{R}}[z,{\overline{z}}]
.

Altogether we get sums of the three factors

\log^{\ell}(z{\overline{z}})(z{\overline{z}})^{M}P(z,{\overline{z}})

for
\ell=0,1,\ldots,{|{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}|}
,
M_{0}\leq M\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and
P\in{\mathbb{R}}[z,{\overline{z}}]
. This gives (
18
) and completes the proof of (G3) in Theorem
5
.

Remark 88
.

With a cutoff in the weights
{\mathbf{n}}
(like
\sum_{e}n_{e}\leq N
) one can use (
110
) to obtain approximations of graphical functions (or Feynman periods).
Particularly efficient is the case when
G\backslash\{0\}
is series-parallel.
With accelerated convergence this gives an algorithm to approximate Feynman periods to many digits. This method was used by Broadhurst and Kreimer in
[
7
]

and later by the second author in
[
34
]
to determine some Feynman periods with an exact numerical approach.
For general graphs there exists the new method of tropical Monte Carlo quadrature by the first author to obtain good approximations
[
4
]
.

21.
Proof of Theorem
49

Lemma 89
.

In polar coordinates
z=Z\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\phi}
we have

\displaystyle D_{z}\equiv\frac{1}{z-{\overline{z}}}(z\partial_{z}-{\overline{z}}\partial_{\overline{z}})=-\frac{1}{2Z\sin\phi}\partial_{\phi}=\frac{1}{Z}\partial_{2\!\cos\phi},

(118)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(\lambda-1)!}\partial_{2\!\cos\phi}^{\lambda-1}\frac{\sin(k+\lambda)\phi}{\sin\phi}=C^{\lambda}_{k}(\cos\phi),

where
\lambda=1,2,3,\ldots
,
k=0,1,2,\ldots
, and
C^{\lambda}_{k}
are the Gegenbauer polynomials (
92
).

Proof.

The first identity follows from
z-{\overline{z}}=2\mathrm{i}Z\sin\phi
and

\partial_{\phi}=\frac{\partial z}{\partial\phi}\partial_{z}+\frac{\partial{\overline{z}}}{\partial\phi}\partial_{\overline{z}}=\mathrm{i}(z\partial_{z}-{\overline{z}}\partial_{\overline{z}})
.

We prove the second identity with generating functions. We multiply both sides with
t^{k+\lambda}
and sum over
k
. It is convenient to extend the sum to negative values
of
k
. To do this we define
C^{\lambda}_{k}=0
for
k<0
. The left hand side gives

\frac{1}{(\lambda-1)!}\partial_{2\!\cos\phi}^{\lambda-1}\sum_{k=-\lambda}^{\infty}\frac{(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\phi}t)^{k+\lambda}-(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\phi}t)^{k+\lambda}}{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\phi}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\phi}}.

The geometric series is
t/(1-(2\cos\phi)t+t^{2})
. Differentiation with
\partial_{2\!\cos\phi}^{\lambda-1}
gives
(\lambda-1)!t^{\lambda}/(1-(2\cos\phi)t+t^{2})^{\lambda}
.
The factor
(\lambda-1)!
cancels and we obtain the generating function of Gegenbauer polynomials (
92
).
∎

Proposition 90
.

The kernel of the differential operator
D_{z}^{\lambda-1}
is

(119)

\ker D_{z}^{\lambda-1}=\sum_{k=0}^{\lambda-2}h_{k}(z{\overline{z}})(z^{k}+{\overline{z}}^{k})

for arbitrary functions
h_{k}(z{\overline{z}})
. In particular, every function in the kernel is symmetric under
z\leftrightarrow{\overline{z}}
.

Proof.

From
2Z\cos\phi=z+{\overline{z}}
and the first identity in (
89
) we obtain that the kernel are polynomials in
z+{\overline{z}}
of degree
\leq\lambda-2
with
z{\overline{z}}
-dependent coefficients.
A linear basis change gives (
119
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
49
assuming Conjecture
86
.

We start by constructing the two-dimensional avatar
{}^{2}\!f_{G}(z)
. We do this in spherical coordinates and use the representation (
110
) of
f_{G}(z)
in terms
of radial and angular graphical functions. The angular graphical function
f_{G(\mathbf{n})\backslash\{0\}}^{\angle}
is a polynomial in
\cos\phi
which can be expanded into
Gegenbauer polynomials by (
112
) where the sum is finite for fixed values of the weights
\mathbf{n}
. We get (for
Z\neq 1
we have absolute convergence)

f_{G}(z)=\Big(\frac{2}{\lambda!}\Big)^{|{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}|}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\mathbf{n}}f_{G(\mathbf{n})}^{R}(Z)f_{G(\mathbf{n})\backslash\{0\},k}^{\angle}C^{\lambda}_{k}(\cos\phi).

We use this expansion to define

(120)

{}^{2}\!f_{G}(z)=\Big(\frac{2}{\lambda!}\Big)^{|{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}|}\frac{2\mathrm{i}Z^{\lambda}}{(\lambda-1)!}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\mathbf{n}}f_{G(\mathbf{n})}^{R}(Z)f_{G(\mathbf{n})\backslash\{0\},k}^{\angle}\sin(k+\lambda)\phi.

Anti-symmetry is provided by anti-symmetry in
\phi
. Equation (
59
) is fulfilled by the orthogonality of the sine,

(121)

\int_{0}^{2\pi}\sin k\phi\sin\ell(\theta-\phi)\mathrm{d}\phi=-\pi\delta_{k,\ell}\cos\theta\quad\text{for }k,\ell=1,2,3,\ldots,

where we set
\theta=0
. To check that
{}^{2}\!f_{G}(z)
fulfills the differential equation (
60
) we divide (
120
) by
z-{\overline{z}}=2\mathrm{i}Z\sin\phi
and use (
89
).

For uniqueness we observe that by Proposition
90
Equation (
60
) determines
{}^{2}\!f^{(\lambda)}_{G}(z)
up to a function

\sum_{k=0}^{\lambda-2}h_{k}(z{\overline{z}})(z^{k}+{\overline{z}}^{k})(z-{\overline{z}})=4\mathrm{i}h_{0}(Z^{2})Z\sin\phi+2\mathrm{i}\sum_{k=1}^{\lambda-2}h_{k}(Z^{2})Z^{k+1}[\sin(k+1)\phi-\sin(k-1)\phi].

Condition (
59
) and orthogonality of the sine (
121
) gives
Z^{k}h_{k-1}(Z^{2})=Z^{k+2}h_{k+1}(Z^{2})
for
k=2,\ldots,\lambda-1
, where we set
h_{\lambda}(Z^{2})=h_{\lambda-1}(Z^{2})=0
.
This gives
h_{\lambda-2}(Z^{2})=\ldots=h_{1}(Z^{2})=0
. Orthogonality for
k=1
gives
h_{0}(Z^{2})=0
. This trivializes the kernel.

Now we prove (
61
). The graph
G
has a Gegenbauer split at an internal vertex
x
, see Definition
47
. The graphical function
f_{G}(z)
is the
Feynman integral
A_{G}
evaluated at special vertices
z_{0}=0
,
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
, see (
11
) and (
12
). It factors in the following sense,

(122)

A_{G}(z_{0},z_{1},z_{2})=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{D}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{D}x}{\pi^{D/2}}A_{G_{1}}(z_{0},z_{1},x)A_{G_{2}}(z_{0},x,z_{2}),

where
G_{i}={\overline{G}}_{i}\backslash\{\infty\}
,
i=1,2
are the de-completed split graphs.
With (
16
) we express the Feynman integrals
A_{G_{i}}
in terms of graphical functions evaluated at invariants (
4
):

\displaystyle A_{G_{1}}(z_{0},z_{1},x)

\displaystyle=f_{G_{1}}(x_{1}),

\displaystyle\text{with $\|x\|^{2}=x_{1}{\overline{x}}_{1}\;$ and $\;\;\|x-1\|^{2}=(x_{1}-1)({\overline{x}}_{1}-1)$},

\displaystyle A_{G_{2}}(z_{0},x,z_{2})

\displaystyle=\|x\|^{-2\lambda N_{G_{2}}}f_{G_{2}}(x_{2}),

\displaystyle\text{with $\frac{z{\overline{z}}}{\|x\|^{2}}=x_{2}{\overline{x}}_{2}$ and $\frac{\|z_{2}-x\|^{2}}{\|x\|^{2}}=(x_{2}-1)({\overline{x}}_{2}-1)$}.

We want to express
f_{G_{i}}(x_{i})
in terms of radial and angular graphical functions. To to this we need polar representations of
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
. From the above identities we find
(up to an irrelevant sign ambiguity in the angle)

x_{1}=X\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\phi_{x1}},\quad\text{and}\quad x_{2}=\frac{Z}{X}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\phi_{xz}},

where
\phi_{x1}
,
\phi_{xz}
are the angles between
x
and
z_{1}
,
z_{2}
, respectively. From (
110
) we get for
f_{G}(z)
the expression

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{2}{\lambda!}\Big)^{{|{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}|}-1}\!\!\!\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{D}x}{\pi^{\lambda+1}}

\displaystyle\Big[\sum_{\mathbf{n}_{1}}f^{R}_{G_{1}(\mathbf{n}_{1})}(X)f^{\angle}_{G_{1}(\mathbf{n}_{1})\backslash\{0\}}(\cos\phi_{x1})\Big]X^{-2\lambda N_{G_{2}}}\times

\displaystyle\times\Big[\sum_{\mathbf{n}_{2}}f^{R}_{G_{2}(\mathbf{n}_{2})}\Big(\!\frac{Z}{X}\!\Big)f^{\angle}_{G_{2}(\mathbf{n}_{2})\backslash\{0\}}(\cos\phi_{xz})\Big].

We expand the angular graphical functions into Gegenbauer polynomials, see (
112
).
With spherical coordinates for
x
we use orthogonality of Gegenbauer polynomials (
95
) to simplify the expression for
f_{G}(z)
to

\Big(\frac{2}{\lambda!}\Big)^{|{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}|}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda C^{\lambda}_{k}(\cos\phi)}{\lambda+k}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}XX^{\alpha}\sum_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}f^{R}_{G_{1}(\mathbf{n}_{1})}(X)f^{\angle}_{G_{1}(\mathbf{n}_{1})\backslash\{0\},k}f^{R}_{G_{2}(\mathbf{n}_{2})}\Big(\!\frac{Z}{X}\!\Big)f^{\angle}_{G_{2}(\mathbf{n}_{2})\backslash\{0\},k},

where
\alpha=2\lambda+1-2\lambda N_{G_{2}}
and
\phi
is the angle between
z
and 1. The two-dimensional avatar (
120
) is obtained by replacing
C^{\lambda}_{k}(\cos\phi)
with

[2\mathrm{i}Z^{\lambda}\sin(k+\lambda)\phi]/(\lambda-1)!
. By
z\partial_{z}-{\overline{z}}\partial_{\overline{z}}=-\mathrm{i}\partial_{\phi}
from the first identity in (
89
) we obtain for the
left hand side of (
61
),

(123)

\Big(\frac{2}{\lambda!}\Big)^{{|{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{int}}}|}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{2\lambda Z^{\lambda}\cos(k+\lambda)\phi}{(\lambda-1)!}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}XX^{\alpha}\sum_{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}}f^{R}_{G_{1}(\mathbf{n}_{1})}(X)f^{\angle}_{G_{1}(\mathbf{n}_{1})\backslash\{0\},k}f^{R}_{G_{2}(\mathbf{n}_{2})}\Big(\frac{Z}{X}\Big)f^{\angle}_{G_{2}(\mathbf{n}_{2})\backslash\{0\},k}.

Convergence of the right hand side of (
61
) is inherited from (
122
). We use polar coordinates
x=X\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta}
,
\mathrm{d}^{2}x=2X\mathrm{d}X\wedge\mathrm{d}\theta
,
and evaluate the integral over
\theta
with (
121
) (and the role of
\phi
and
\theta
interchanged). The result reproduces (
123
).

For uniqueness of
{}^{2}\!f_{G}(z)
in (
61
) we observe that the kernel of
z\partial_{z}-{\overline{z}}\partial_{\overline{z}}
are radial functions
h(z{\overline{z}})
, see the case
\lambda=2
in
Proposition
90
. Asymmetry of
{}^{2}\!f_{G}(z)
therefore kills the kernel.
∎

Proof of Proposition
51
.

From uniqueness of
{}^{2}\!f_{G}(z)
and (
120
) it is clear that
{}^{2}\!f_{G}(z)
is analytic in
z
and
{\overline{z}}
for
1\neq Z\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
with single-valued log-Laurent expansions at 0 and
\infty
.

We fix a point
a
on the unit circle. We want to use (
60
) to determine
{}^{2}\!f_{G}(z)
by
(\lambda-1)
times inverting
D_{z}
.
Assume
g(z)=g({\overline{z}})\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}
. We show that in the neighborhood of
z=a
there exists a symmetric solution of
D_{z}f(z)=g(z)
which is analytic in
z
and
{\overline{z}}

if
a\neq 1
or has a single-valued log-Laurent expansion if
a=1
. To do this we locally invert the differential operator
D_{z}=-(2Z\sin\phi)^{-1}\partial_{\phi}
at
z=a
.

We first consider the case
a\neq 1
. By linearity it suffices to handle the monomial
(z-a)^{m}({\overline{z}}-{\overline{a}})^{\overline{m}}
.
We multiply the monomial with
\mathrm{i}(z-{\overline{z}})=-2Z\sin\phi
and expand the expression. We obtain a sum of terms

\mathrm{i}Z^{k+{\overline{k}}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(k-{\overline{k}})\phi}
with
k\leq m+1
,
{\overline{k}}\leq{\overline{m}}+1
. To invert the differential operator
\partial_{\phi}
we integrate over the variable
\phi
.
We get
z^{k}{\overline{z}}^{\overline{k}}/(k-{\overline{k}})
if
k\neq{\overline{k}}
and
(z{\overline{z}})^{k}\log z
otherwise (fix any branch of the logarithm). Both terms are analytic in
z
and
{\overline{z}}
at
z=a
,
{\overline{z}}={\overline{a}}
.
The symmetry of
f(z)
follows from the symmetry of
D_{z}
(see (
119
) for
\lambda=2
).

We are left with the case
a=1
. By the symmetry of
g(z)
the expansion (
18
) at
z=1
can be organized in terms of symmetric monomials

(124)

\frac{\log^{\ell}[(z-1)({\overline{z}}-1)]}{[(z-1)({\overline{z}}-1)]^{-M_{1}}}\left[(z-1)^{k}({\overline{z}}-1)^{\overline{k}}+({\overline{z}}-1)^{k}(z-1)^{\overline{k}}\right]

for
M_{1}\leq 0
and
k,{\overline{k}}\geq 0
. The differential operator
D_{z}
lowers the total degree of symmetric polynomials by 1:

(125)

D_{z}(z^{k}{\overline{z}}^{\overline{k}}+{\overline{z}}^{k}z^{\overline{k}})=(k-{\overline{k}})\frac{z^{k}{\overline{z}}^{\overline{k}}-{\overline{z}}^{k}z^{\overline{k}}}{z-{\overline{z}}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[z,{\overline{z}}],

which is of total degree
k+{\overline{k}}-1
. On functions of
|z-1|^{2}
the operator
D_{z}
is a negative derivative:

D_{z}F((z-1)({\overline{z}}-1))=-F^{\prime}((z-1)({\overline{z}}-1)).

We combine both facts to invert
D_{z}
on (
124
) using integration by parts. The first factor is integrated whereas the second factor is differentiated.
We repeat integrating by parts until the second factor is zero. The result provides the single-valued log-Laurent expansion of
f(z)
at
z=1
.

By repeated application of this result and Proposition
90
we obtain that
{}^{2}\!f_{G}(z)/(z-{\overline{z}})
differs from a function with good expansions at the unit circle by
a function
h(z)=\sum_{k=0}^{\lambda-2}h_{k}(z{\overline{z}})(z^{k}+{\overline{z}}^{k})
. By real-analyticity of
{}^{2}\!f_{G}(z)/(z-{\overline{z}})
at some point
a\neq 1
on the unit circle we obtain
that
h(z)
is analytic in
z
and
{\overline{z}}
at
z=a
,
{\overline{z}}={\overline{a}}
. Hence, all
h_{k}(z{\overline{z}})
are regular at 1. Therefore
h(z)
is analytic in
z
and
{\overline{z}}
on the whole unit circle and the result follows.
∎

22.
Proof of Theorem
33

Let
\lambda-1=n=0,1,2,\ldots
. In this section we determine the kernel of
\Delta_{n}=\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{z}}+n(n+1)/(z-{\overline{z}})^{2}
in (
10
).

Lemma 91
.

With

(126)

\mathcal{D}_{n}=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\frac{(-1)^{k}n!(k+\ell)!}{(n-k)!(k-\ell)!\ell!}(\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{z}})^{n-k}(\partial_{z}-\partial_{\overline{z}})^{k-\ell}(z-{\overline{z}})^{n-k-\ell}

and

d_{n}=\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{k}\frac{(n+k)!}{(n-k)!k!}\frac{1}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{k}}\partial_{z}^{n-k}

(see (
6.1
)) we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{n}\Delta_{n}

\displaystyle=(\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{z}})^{n+1}(z-{\overline{z}})^{n},

(127)

\displaystyle\Delta_{n}d_{n}

\displaystyle=\partial_{z}d_{n}\partial_{\overline{z}}.

Proof.

For the first identity in (
91
) we use

(128)

(z-{\overline{z}})^{k}\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{z}}=\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{z}}(z-{\overline{z}})^{k}+k(\partial_{z}-\partial_{\overline{z}})(z-{\overline{z}})^{k-1}-k(k-1)(z-{\overline{z}})^{k-2},

which follows by applying the Leibniz rule to the right hand side.

Substituting the above equation into
\mathcal{D}_{n}\Delta_{n}
gives three terms:

\displaystyle\sum_{k=-1}^{n-1}\sum_{\ell=-1}^{k}\frac{(-1)^{k+1}n!(k+\ell+2)!}{(n-k-1)!(k-\ell)!(\ell+1)!}(\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{z}})^{n-k}(\partial_{z}-\partial_{\overline{z}})^{k-\ell}(z-{\overline{z}})^{n-k-\ell-2}

\displaystyle+\;\sum_{k=0}^{n}\sum_{\ell=-1}^{k-1}\frac{(-1)^{k}n!(k+\ell+1)!(n-k-\ell-1)}{(n-k)!(k-\ell-1)!(\ell+1)!}(\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{z}})^{n-k}(\partial_{z}-\partial_{\overline{z}})^{k-\ell}(z-{\overline{z}})^{n-k-\ell-2}

(129)

\displaystyle+\;\sum_{k=0}^{n}\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\frac{(-1)^{k}n!(k+\ell)!(2n-k-\ell)(k+\ell+1)}{(n-k)!(k-\ell)!\ell!}(\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{z}})^{n-k}(\partial_{z}-\partial_{\overline{z}})^{k-\ell}(z-{\overline{z}})^{n-k-\ell-2},

where in the first term we shifted
k\mapsto k+1
,
\ell\mapsto\ell+1
, in the second term we shifted
\ell\mapsto\ell+1
, and in the last term we combined the third term on the right hand
side of (
128
) with the second term of
\Delta_{n}
.

The three summands in (
22
) add up to zero. We are left with boundary terms
k,\ell=-1
and
k=n
,
\ell=k
. The boundary terms are

\displaystyle(\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{z}})^{n+1}(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}+\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{(-1)^{k+1}n!}{(n-k-1)!}(\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{z}})^{n-k}(\partial_{z}-\partial_{\overline{z}})^{k+1}(z-{\overline{z}})^{n-k-1}

\displaystyle+\;\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{(-1)^{k}n!}{(n-k-1)!}(\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{z}})^{n-k}(\partial_{z}-\partial_{\overline{z}})^{k+1}(z-{\overline{z}})^{n-k-1}\quad=\quad(\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{z}})^{n+1}(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}

which proves the first identity in (
91
). For the second identity in (
91
) we calculate

\partial_{\overline{z}}d_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k}\frac{(n+k)!}{(n-k)!(k-1)!}\frac{1}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{k+1}}\partial_{z}^{n-k}+d_{n}\partial_{\overline{z}}.

Applying
\partial_{z}
to the first term on the right hand side gives two terms, one when
\partial_{z}
hits
z-{\overline{z}}
and the second one when
\partial_{z}
commutes to the right.
In the second term we shift
k
to
k+1
and obtain for the sum of both terms

\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{k+1}\frac{(n+k)![k(k+1)+(n+k+1)(n-k)]}{(n-k)!k!}\frac{1}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{k+2}}\partial_{z}^{n-k}=-\frac{n(n+1)}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{2}}d_{n}.

This term cancels the contribution from the second term in
\Delta_{n}
. We obtain the second identity in (
91
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
33
.

From the second identity in (
91
) we get
\Delta_{n}d_{n}h(z)=\partial_{z}d_{n}\partial_{\overline{z}}h(z)=0
. So,
d_{n}h(z)
is in the kernel of
\Delta_{n}
for any holomorphic function
h(z)
.
By complex conjugation we get
\Delta_{n}{\overline{d}}_{n}{\overline{h}}({\overline{z}})=0
.

We need to show that
d_{n}h(z)+{\overline{d}}_{n}{\overline{h}}({\overline{z}})
exhausts the kernel of
\Delta_{n}
.
To do this we use the first identity in (
91
) to see that any function
F
in the kernel of
\Delta_{n}
fulfills the identity

(\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{z}})^{n+1}(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}F=0.

By repeated integration the kernel of
(\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{z}})^{n+1}
are polynomials in
z
of degree
\leq n
with anti-holomorphic coefficients plus polynomials in
{\overline{z}}
of degree
\leq n

with holomorphic coefficients. Therefore, there are (independent) (anti-)holomorphic functions
h_{k}(z)
(
{\overline{h}}_{k}({\overline{z}})
) such that
(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}F(z,{\overline{z}})=\sum_{k=0}^{n}h_{k}(z){\overline{z}}^{k}+{\overline{h}}_{k}({\overline{z}})z^{k}
.
By a linear transformation we equivalently have (anti-)holomorphic functions
f_{k}(z)
(
{\overline{f}}_{k}({\overline{z}})
) with

(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}F(z,{\overline{z}})=\sum_{k=0}^{n}(f_{n-k}(z)+{\overline{f}}_{n-k}({\overline{z}}))(z-{\overline{z}})^{k}
. We get

F=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{f_{k}(z)+{\overline{f}}_{k}({\overline{z}})}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{k}}.

With

h(z)=\frac{(-1)^{n}n!}{(2n)!}f_{n}(z)\quad\text{and}\quad{\overline{h}}({\overline{z}})=\frac{n!}{(2n)!}{\overline{f}}_{n}({\overline{z}})

we obtain

F=d_{n}h(z)+{\overline{d}}_{n}{\overline{h}}({\overline{z}})+\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{f_{k}(z)+{\overline{f}}_{k}({\overline{z}})-\frac{(n+k)!n!}{(n-k)!k!(2n)!}[(-1)^{n-k}\partial_{z}^{n-k}f_{n}(z)+\partial_{\overline{z}}^{n-k}{\overline{f}}_{n}({\overline{z}})]}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{k}}.

The term
k=n
is zero. For
n=0
this proves the theorem, so we assume
n\geq 1
in the following.
Introducing new (anti-)homomorphic functions
g_{k}(z)
(
{\overline{g}}_{k}({\overline{z}})
) we get that

F=d_{n}h(z)+{\overline{d}}_{n}{\overline{h}}({\overline{z}})+G_{n-1}

where

(130)

G_{m}=\sum_{k=0}^{m}\frac{g_{k}(z)+{\overline{g}}_{k}({\overline{z}})}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{k}}.

We have
\Delta_{n}F=\Delta_{n}G_{n-1}=0
. We show by induction that
\Delta_{n}G_{m}=0
implies
G_{m}=0
for all
m=0,\ldots,n-1
.

From
\Delta_{n}G_{m}=0
we get

\sum_{k=0}^{m}\left(k\frac{g_{k}^{\prime}(z)-{\overline{g}}_{k}^{\prime}({\overline{z}})}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{k+1}}+[n(n+1)-k(k+1)]\frac{g_{k}(z)+{\overline{g}}_{k}({\overline{z}})}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{k+2}}\right)=0,

with
n(n+1)-k(k+1)=(n-k)(n+k+1)
. For
m=0
the above equation implies
G_{0}=0
. We assume
m>0
and multiply the above equation with
(z-{\overline{z}})^{2}/(n-m)(n+m+1)
.
In the first term we also shift the summation index
k
to
k+1
and obtain

\frac{g_{m}(z)+{\overline{g}}_{m}({\overline{z}})}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{m}}=-\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\frac{(k+1)(g_{k+1}^{\prime}(z)-{\overline{g}}_{k+1}^{\prime}({\overline{z}}))+(n-k)(n+k+1)(g_{k}(z)+{\overline{g}}_{k}({\overline{z}}))}{(n-m)(n+m+1)(z-{\overline{z}})^{k}}.

Substitution into (
130
) gives

G_{m}=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\frac{-(k+1)(g_{k+1}^{\prime}(z)-{\overline{g}}_{k+1}^{\prime}({\overline{z}}))-(m-k)(m+k+1)(g_{k}(z)+{\overline{g}}_{k}({\overline{z}}))}{(n-m)(n+m+1)(z-{\overline{z}})^{k}}.

The right hand side is
G_{m-1}
for some new functions
\widetilde{g}_{k}(z)
,
\widetilde{{\overline{g}}}_{k}({\overline{z}})
and vanishes by induction.
∎

23.
Proof of Theorem
34

Lemma 92
.

For non-negative integers
a,b,c
we have

(131)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \sum_{k=0}^{c}(-1)^{k}\frac{(b+k)!}{(c-k)!k!(k+b-a)!}&=(-1)^{c}\frac{a!b!}{(a-c)!(b+c-a)!c!},\\
\sum_{k=0}^{c}(-1)^{k}\frac{(b+k)!}{(c-k)!k!(k+b+a)!}&=\frac{(a+c-1)!b!}{(a-1)!(a+b+c)!c!},\end{aligned}

where terms with negative factorials in the denominator vanish. For
m\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
we have

(132)

\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k}\frac{(n+k)!}{(n-k)!k!(k-1)!(k+m)}=(-1)^{n}-\delta_{m,0},

where
\delta_{k,\ell}
is the Kronecker delta.

Proof.

For the first identity in (
131
) we calculate the
x^{a}
-coefficient of

\sum_{k=0}^{c}(-1)^{k}\genfrac{(}{)}{0.0pt}{}{c}{k}(1+x)^{b+k}=(1-(1+x))^{c}(1+x)^{b}=(-x)^{c}(1+x)^{b}.

For the second identity in (
131
) we replace factorials which depend on
a
by the gamma function according to
x!=\Gamma(x+1)
.
We analytically continue to negative
a
. The reflection formula
\Gamma(x)\Gamma(1-x)=\pi/\sin(\pi x)
gives the second identity because

\sin\pi(a-c+1)=(-1)^{c}\sin\pi(a+1)
.

For (
132
) we calculate

\int_{0}^{1}x^{m}\frac{\partial^{n+1}}{\partial x^{n+1}}x^{n}(1-x)^{n}\mathrm{d}x=\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{k}\genfrac{(}{)}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}\int_{0}^{1}x^{m}\frac{\partial^{n+1}}{\partial x^{n+1}}x^{n+k}\mathrm{d}x=\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{k}\genfrac{(}{)}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}\frac{(n+k)!}{(k-1)!(k+m)}.

The left hand side can also be calculated by using integration by parts
n+1
times. Because
n+1>m
the final integral is zero. Only the first boundary term gives a non-zero
result which is

\displaystyle x^{m}\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial x^{n}}x^{n}(1-x)^{n}\Big|_{0}^{1}=(-1)^{n}n!-\delta_{m,0}\,n!\qed

Lemma 93
.

We have (see (
6.1
),
\sum_{k=1}^{0}\equiv 0
)

(133)

\Delta_{n}I^{-}_{n}=\partial_{z}I^{-}_{n}\partial_{\overline{z}}-\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{n-k}\frac{(n+k)!}{(n-k)!k!(k-1)!}\frac{1}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{k+1}}\left[\partial_{\overline{z}},\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}z\right](z-{\overline{z}})^{k}\quad\text{on }\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}.

If
n\geq 1
, there exist anti-holomorphic functions
{\overline{g}}_{k}({\overline{z}})
on
{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}
for
k=1,\ldots,n
such that

(134)

\partial_{z}I^{-}_{n}\partial_{z}I^{+}_{n}=1+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{{\overline{g}}_{k}({\overline{z}})}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{k+1}}\quad\text{on }(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}.

Proof.

Differentiation
\partial_{z}
of
I^{\pm}_{n}
in (
6.1
) gives two terms. If
\partial_{z}
hits
\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}z
, we obtain 1 from the first identity in (
131
) for
a=b=c=n
.
The second term comes from differentiating
(z-{\overline{z}})^{\pm k}
. We get

(135)

\partial_{z}I^{\pm}_{n}=1\pm\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{n-k}\frac{(n+k)!}{(n-k)!k!(k-1)!}(z-{\overline{z}})^{\pm k-1}\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}z\;(z-{\overline{z}})^{\mp k}

on the corresponding function spaces.

For (
133
) we commute
\partial_{\overline{z}}
with
\partial_{z}I^{-}_{n}
.
We obtain two contributions from differentiating
z-{\overline{z}}
before and after the integral
\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}z
. In the latter term we shift the summation index
k
to
k+1
to obtain
for the sum of both terms

-\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{n-k}\frac{(n+k)![k(k+1)+(n+k+1)(n-k)]}{(n-k)!k!^{2}}\frac{1}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{k+2}}\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}z\;(z-{\overline{z}})^{k}=-\frac{n(n+1)}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{2}}I^{-}_{n}.

This gives the result.

For (
134
) we first note that
I^{+}_{n}
maps
(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}
to
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}
which is stable under
\partial_{z}
.
Hence the left hand side is well-defined. We use (
135
) to express
-(\partial_{z}I^{-}_{n}-1)(\partial_{z}I^{+}_{n}-1)
as the double sum

\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{n}(-1)^{k+\ell}\frac{(n+k)!}{(n-k)!k!(k-1)!}\frac{(n+\ell)!}{(n-\ell)!\ell!(\ell-1)!}\frac{1}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{k+1}}\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}z\;(z-{\overline{z}})^{k+\ell-1}\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}z\frac{1}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{\ell}}.

Integration by parts gives for
k+\ell\neq 0
,

\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}z\;(z-{\overline{z}})^{k+\ell-1}\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}z\frac{1}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{\ell}}=\frac{1}{k+\ell}\left[(z-{\overline{z}})^{k+\ell},\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}z\right]\frac{1}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{\ell}}+{\overline{h}}_{k+\ell}({\overline{z}})

with an anti-holomorphic function
{\overline{h}}_{k+\ell}({\overline{z}})
on
{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}
.
We substitute this into the previous equation and use (
132
) for
m=\ell\geq 1
and
m=k\geq 1
.
With (
135
) this gives

\displaystyle-(\partial_{z}I^{-}_{n}-1)(\partial_{z}I^{+}_{n}-1)=

\displaystyle=\partial_{z}I^{+}-1+\partial_{z}I^{-}-1+\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{n}(-1)^{k+\ell}\frac{(n+k)!}{(n-k)!k!(k-1)!}\frac{(n+\ell)!}{(n-\ell)!\ell!(\ell-1)!}\frac{{\overline{h}}_{k+\ell}({\overline{z}})}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{k+1}}.

A linear transformation of the functions
{\overline{h}}_{\bullet}({\overline{z}})
leads to (
134
).
∎

Lemma 94
.

Let
f(z)\in(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}
. We have
\Delta_{0}\mathcal{I}_{0}f(z)=f(z)
(
\mathcal{I}_{n}
is defined in (
47
)).
If
n\geq 1
, there exist anti-holomorphic functions
{\overline{g}}_{k}({\overline{z}})
on
{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}
for
k=1,\ldots,n
such that

(136)

\Delta_{n}\mathcal{I}_{n}f(z)=f(z)+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{{\overline{g}}_{k}({\overline{z}})}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{k+1}}.

Proof.

We have
\mathcal{I}_{0}=\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}z\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}{\overline{z}}
. Hence
\Delta_{0}\mathcal{I}_{0}=1
. Now, assume
n\geq 1
.
Because
\partial_{z}[\partial_{\overline{z}},\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}z]=\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{z}}\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}z-\partial_{\overline{z}}=0
the commutator
[\partial_{\overline{z}},\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}z]
projects onto
anti-holomorphic functions. We define

{\overline{h}}_{k}({\overline{z}})=(-1)^{n-k+1}\frac{(n+k)!}{(n-k)!k!(k-1)!}\left[\partial_{\overline{z}},\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}z\right](z-{\overline{z}})^{k}\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}{\overline{z}}\,\partial_{z}I^{+}_{n}f(z).

With (
133
) we obtain

\Delta_{n}\mathcal{I}_{n}f(z)=\partial_{z}I^{-}_{n}\partial_{\overline{z}}\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}{\overline{z}}\,\partial_{z}I^{+}_{n}f(z)+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{{\overline{h}}_{k}({\overline{z}})}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{k+1}}.

The result follows from (
134
) after a re-definition of
{\overline{g}}_{k}({\overline{z}})+{\overline{h}}_{k}({\overline{z}})
as
{\overline{g}}_{k}({\overline{z}})
.
∎

Lemma 95
.

For any anti-holomorphic function
{\overline{g}}_{k}({\overline{z}})
on
{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}
and
k=1,\ldots,n
we have (see (
6.1
))

(137)

\Delta_{n}D_{n}\frac{{\overline{g}}_{k}({\overline{z}})}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{k+1}}=\frac{{\overline{g}}_{k}({\overline{z}})}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{k+1}}.

Proof.

The differential operator
\partial_{z}
in
D_{n}
can only act on
z-{\overline{z}}
,

D_{n}\frac{{\overline{g}}_{k}({\overline{z}})}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{k+1}}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}(-1)^{\ell-1}\frac{(n-\ell)!}{(n+\ell)!}(z-{\overline{z}})^{\ell}\partial_{\overline{z}}^{\ell-1}{\overline{g}}_{k}({\overline{z}})\partial_{z}^{\ell-1}(z-{\overline{z}})^{\ell-k-1}.

Terms with
\ell\geq k+1\geq 2
are nullified by
\partial_{z}^{\ell-1}
. This gives

(138)

D_{n}\frac{{\overline{g}}_{k}({\overline{z}})}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{k+1}}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\frac{(n-\ell)!(k-1)!}{(n+\ell)!(k-\ell)!}(z-{\overline{z}})^{\ell}\partial_{\overline{z}}^{\ell-1}\frac{{\overline{g}}_{k}({\overline{z}})}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{k}}.

We get

\partial_{z}D_{n}\frac{{\overline{g}}_{k}({\overline{z}})}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{k+1}}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\frac{(n-\ell)!(k-1)!}{(n+\ell)!(k-\ell)!}\left[\ell(z-{\overline{z}})^{\ell-1}\partial_{\overline{z}}^{\ell-1}(z-{\overline{z}})-k(z-{\overline{z}})^{\ell}\partial_{\overline{z}}^{\ell-1}\right]\frac{{\overline{g}}_{k}({\overline{z}})}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{k+1}}.

The square bracket equals
-(k-\ell)(z-{\overline{z}})^{\ell}\partial_{\overline{z}}^{\ell-1}-\ell(\ell-1)(z-{\overline{z}})^{\ell-1}\partial_{\overline{z}}^{\ell-2}
.
We substitute this into the previous equation and shift
\ell\mapsto\ell-1
in the first term. From the identity
-(n+\ell)(n-\ell+1)-\ell(\ell-1)=-n(n+1)
we get

(139)

\partial_{z}D_{n}\frac{{\overline{g}}_{k}({\overline{z}})}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{k+1}}=-n(n+1)\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}\frac{(n-\ell)!(k-1)!}{(n+\ell)!(k-\ell)!}(z-{\overline{z}})^{\ell-1}\partial_{\overline{z}}^{\ell-2}\frac{{\overline{g}}_{k}({\overline{z}})}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{k+1}}.

We have

\partial_{\overline{z}}(z-{\overline{z}})^{\ell-1}\partial_{\overline{z}}^{\ell-2}=(z-{\overline{z}})^{\ell-2}\partial_{\overline{z}}^{\ell-1}(z-{\overline{z}})

because both sides equal
-(\ell-1)(z-{\overline{z}})^{\ell-2}\partial_{\overline{z}}^{\ell-2}+(z-{\overline{z}})^{\ell-1}\partial_{\overline{z}}^{\ell-1}
.
We use this identity to calculate
\partial_{\overline{z}}\partial_{z}D_{n}{\overline{g}}_{k}({\overline{z}})(z-{\overline{z}})^{-k-1}
from (
139
) and get with (
138
)

\partial_{\overline{z}}\partial_{z}D_{n}\frac{{\overline{g}}_{k}({\overline{z}})}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{k+1}}=-\frac{n(n+1)}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{2}}\left[D_{n}-\frac{(z-{\overline{z}})^{2}}{n(n+1)}\right]\frac{{\overline{g}}_{k}({\overline{z}})}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{k+1}},

where the second term in the square bracket cancels the
\ell=1
summand in (
138
). Equation (
137
) follows.
∎

Lemma 96
.

For any (anti-)holomorphic functions
h(z)
,
{\overline{h}}({\overline{z}})
on
{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}
we have (see (
6.1
))

(140)

\partial_{\overline{z}}^{n}\partial_{z}^{n+1}(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}[d_{n}h(z)+{\overline{d}}_{n}{\overline{h}}({\overline{z}})]=(-1)^{n}n!\partial_{z}^{2n+1}h(z).

For
m\in\{0,1,\ldots,2n\}
we have

(141)

d_{n}z^{m}=(-1)^{n}{\overline{d}}_{n}{\overline{z}}^{m}.

Let
p\in{\mathbb{C}}[z]
be of degree
\leq 2n
. Then
(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}d_{n}p(z)
is a symmetric polynomial of degree
\leq n
in
z
and
{\overline{z}}
.

Proof.

Because
\partial_{z}^{n+1}(z-{\overline{z}})^{n-k}=0
at least one derivative
\partial_{z}
hits
{\overline{h}}({\overline{z}})
, nullifying it. So, we can assume
{\overline{h}}({\overline{z}})=0
.
Then, the only anti-holomorphic term in
d_{k}
is
(z-{\overline{z}})^{n-k}
and
\partial_{\overline{z}}^{n}(z-{\overline{z}})^{n-k}=(-1)^{n}n!\delta_{k,0}
. The sum over
k
becomes trivial and (
140
) follows.

To prove (
141
) we calculate

\displaystyle(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}d_{n}z^{m}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{k}\frac{(n+k)!m!(z-{\overline{z}})^{n-k}z^{m-n+k}}{(n-k)!k!(m-n+k)!}=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-k}\frac{(-1)^{k+\ell}(n+k)!m!z^{m-\ell}{\overline{z}}^{\ell}}{k!(m-n+k)!(n-k-\ell)!\ell!}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}(-1)^{\ell}\frac{m!z^{m-\ell}{\overline{z}}^{\ell}}{\ell!}\sum_{k=0}^{n-\ell}(-1)^{k}\frac{(n+k)!}{(n-\ell-k)!k!(k+m-n)!},

where terms with negative factorials in the denominator vanish.
We substitute
a=2n-m
,
b=n
,
c=n-\ell
into the first identity of (
131
) and obtain

\displaystyle(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}d_{n}z^{m}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}(-1)^{\ell}\frac{m!z^{m-\ell}{\overline{z}}^{\ell}}{\ell!}\frac{(-1)^{n-\ell}(2n-m)!n!}{(n-m+\ell)!(m-\ell)!(n-\ell)!}=

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}n!\sum_{\ell=0}^{m}\genfrac{(}{)}{0.0pt}{}{m}{\ell}\genfrac{(}{)}{0.0pt}{}{2n-m}{n-\ell}z^{m-\ell}{\overline{z}}^{\ell}.

By substituting
\ell\mapsto m-\ell
we see that the result is symmetric under complex conjugation. Upon division by
(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}
we obtain (
141
).

For the last statement we observe that
P=(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}d_{n}p(z)
is a polynomial in
z
and
{\overline{z}}
. Because
p
has degree
\leq 2n
we obtain from (
141
) that
P
is symmetric
under
z\leftrightarrow{\overline{z}}
. Obviously
P
is of degree
\leq n
in
{\overline{z}}
. By symmetry it is also of degree
\leq n
in
z
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
34
.

With
F(z)
in (
48
) we calculate
\Delta_{n}F(z)
:

By Theorem
33
we have
\Delta_{n}(d_{n}h(z)+{\overline{d}}_{n}{\overline{h}}({\overline{z}}))=0
.

From Lemma
94
we get
\Delta_{n}\mathcal{I}_{n}f(z)=f(z)+\sum_{k=1}^{n}{\overline{g}}_{k}({\overline{z}})/(z-{\overline{z}})^{k+1}
for some anti-holomorphic functions
{\overline{g}}_{k}({\overline{z}})
.
Hence
(1-\Delta_{n}\mathcal{I}_{n})f(z)=-\sum_{k=1}^{n}{\overline{g}}_{k}({\overline{z}})/(z-{\overline{z}})^{k+1}
and by Lemma
95
we get

\Delta_{n}D_{n}(1-\Delta_{n}\mathcal{I}_{n})f(z)=f(z)-\Delta_{n}\mathcal{I}_{n}f(z).

This gives
\Delta_{n}F(z)=f(z)
.

We need to show that for a suitable choice of
\phi
,
\overline{\phi}
,
p_{0}
,
p_{1}
every such
F\in(z-{\overline{z}})^{-n}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}
is given by the right hand side of (
48
).
It follows from Theorem
33
that for any function
F(z)
with
\Delta_{n}F(z)=f(z)
there exist (anti-)holomorphic functions
h(z)
,
{\overline{h}}({\overline{z}})
on
{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}
such
that (
48
) holds. By (
46
) we get

(142)

(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}[d_{n}h(z)+{\overline{d}}_{n}{\overline{h}}({\overline{z}})]\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}.

Because
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}
is stable under (anti-)differentiation we get from Lemma
96
that
\partial_{z}^{2n+1}h(z)\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}
.
Hence
\partial_{z}^{2n+1}h(z)
has single-valued log-Laurent expansions (
18
), (
20
) at
0,1,\infty
. Because
h(z)
is analytic we get that
\partial_{z}^{2n+1}h(z)
is
meromorphic on the Riemann sphere with poles at
0,1,\infty
. By the theory of Riemann surfaces we get
\partial_{z}^{2n+1}h(z)\in{\mathbb{C}}[z,z^{-1},(z-1)^{-1}]
.
We use partial fraction decomposition to see that
h(z)=h_{0}(z)\log(z)+h_{1}(z)\log(z-1)+\phi(z)
with
h_{0},h_{1}\in{\mathbb{C}}[z]
of degrees
\leq 2n
and
\phi\in{\mathbb{C}}[z,z^{-1},(z-1)^{-1}]
.

We get the complex conjugated result for
{\overline{h}}({\overline{z}})
and substitute this into (
142
). By single-valuedness the result is of the form

H_{0}\log(z{\overline{z}})+H_{1}\log[(z-1)({\overline{z}}-1)]+\Phi
with
H_{0},H_{1}\in{\mathbb{C}}[z,{\overline{z}}]
and
\Phi\in{\mathbb{C}}[z,z^{-1},(z-1)^{-1},{\overline{z}},{\overline{z}}^{-1},({\overline{z}}-1)^{-1}]
.
The polynomials
H_{0}
and
H_{1}
are obtained when no differential operator acts on logarithms in
h
and
{\overline{h}}
. We get

(143)

H_{i}=(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}d_{n}h_{i}(z)=(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}{\overline{d}}_{n}{\overline{h}}_{i}({\overline{z}}),\quad\text{for }i=0,1.

If
{\overline{h}}_{i}({\overline{z}})=(-1)^{n}h_{i}({\overline{z}})
, then, by (
141
), we have
{\overline{d}}_{n}{\overline{h}}_{i}({\overline{z}})=(-1)^{n}{\overline{d}}_{n}h_{i}({\overline{z}})=d_{n}h_{i}(z)
and (
143
) is fulfilled.

We need to show that (
143
) fixes the choice of
{\overline{h}}_{i}({\overline{z}})
. Then (
49
) follows and the proof of Theorem
34
is complete.
We consider
z
and
{\overline{z}}
as independent variables. The pole of order
n
at
z={\overline{z}}
in
{\overline{d}}_{n}{\overline{h}}_{i}({\overline{z}})
is
(-1)^{n}(2n)!{\overline{h}}_{i}({\overline{z}})/(n!(z-{\overline{z}})^{n})
.
This allows one to determine
{\overline{h}}_{i}({\overline{z}})
from
{\overline{d}}_{n}{\overline{h}}_{i}({\overline{z}})
and the claim follows.
∎

In Theorem
34
the definition of the integral operator
\mathcal{I}_{n}
has three integrations: Two with respect to
\mathrm{d}z
in
I_{n}^{-},I_{n}^{+}
and one explicit integration with respect to
\mathrm{d}{\overline{z}}
.
We close the section with an alternative representation
\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime}
which has only two integrations: one holomorphic and one anti-holomorphic.
We need the result for the proof of Theorem
45
on the weights of constructible graphical functions. For implementing the algorithm of appending an edge
the operator
\mathcal{I}_{n}
is more efficient.

Lemma 97
.

Let
\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime}:(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}\rightarrow(z-{\overline{z}})^{-n}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}
be defined by

(144)

\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime}=\sum_{k,\ell=0}^{n}(-1)^{n+k+\ell}\frac{(n+k)!(n+\ell)!}{(n-k)!(n-\ell)!(k+\ell)!k!\ell!}\frac{1}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{k}}\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}z(z-{\overline{z}})^{k+\ell}\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}{\overline{z}}\frac{1}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{\ell}}.

Then Lemma
94
holds if
\mathcal{I}_{n}
is replaced by
\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We have
\mathcal{I}_{0}^{\prime}=\mathcal{I}_{0}
and hence assume that
n\geq 1
.

The derivative
\partial_{z}
generates two terms on the right hand of (
144
). In one term
(z-{\overline{z}})^{-k}
is differentiated and in the second term the integral operator
\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}z

is annihilated. After the annihilation of
\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}z
the sum over
k
can be evaluated with the first identity in (
131
) for
a=n-\ell
,
b=c=n
.
The result
(-1)^{n}\delta_{\ell,0}
trivializes the sum over
\ell
and the second term becomes
\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}{\overline{z}}
,

(145)

\partial_{z}\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}\frac{-(-1)^{n+k+\ell}(n+k)!(n+\ell)!}{(n-k)!(n-\ell)!(k+\ell)!(k-1)!\ell!}\frac{1}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{k+1}}\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}z(z-{\overline{z}})^{k+\ell}\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}{\overline{z}}\frac{1}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{\ell}}+\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}{\overline{z}}.

The derivative
\partial_{\overline{z}}
on the first term of the right hand side gives four terms. Term one and three hit powers of
z-{\overline{z}}
.
The second term has the commutator
[\partial_{\overline{z}},\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}z]
which gives rise to the anti-holomorphic functions
{\overline{g}}_{k}({\overline{z}})
on the right hand side of (
136
).
The fourth term annihilates
\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}{\overline{z}}
. Like above we get in this term
(-1)^{n}\delta_{k,0}
from evaluating the sum over
\ell
.
The
k
-sum starts at 1, so the fourth term is zero. After a shift
k\mapsto k+1
the third term adds to the first term yielding

-\sum_{k,\ell=0}^{n}\frac{(-1)^{n+k+\ell}[k(k+1)+(n+k+1)(n-k)](n+k)!(n+\ell)!}{(n-k)!(n-\ell)!(k+\ell)!k!\ell!\;(z-{\overline{z}})^{k+2}}\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}z(z-{\overline{z}})^{k+\ell}\int_{\mathrm{sv}}\mathrm{d}{\overline{z}}\frac{1}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{\ell}}.

This is
-n(n+1)(z-{\overline{z}})^{-2}\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime}
and the result follows because the second term in (
145
) differentiates to the identity.
∎

24.
Proof of Theorem
36

Let
n=0,1,2,\ldots
. Assume
f(z)
with (G3) for the graph
G_{1}
is in the kernel of
\Delta_{n}(z-{\overline{z}})^{n+1}
. We need to show
f(z)=0
.

We consider
(z-{\overline{z}})^{2n+1}f(z)
. From Theorem
33
we know that there exist (anti-)holomorphic functions
h(z)
,
{\overline{h}}({\overline{z}})
on
{\mathbb{C}}\backslash{\mathbb{R}}
such that

(146)

(z-{\overline{z}})^{2n+1}f(z)=(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}[d_{n}h(z)+{\overline{d}}_{n}{\overline{h}}({\overline{z}})].

By (G3) we have
f\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}
. We are thus in the situation of (
142
) in the proof of Theorem
34
.
We follow the proof to conclude that there exist (anti-)meromorphic functions
\phi\in{\mathbb{C}}[z,z^{-1},(z-1)^{-1}]
,
\overline{\phi}\in{\mathbb{C}}[{\overline{z}},{\overline{z}}^{-1},({\overline{z}}-1)^{-1}]

and polynomials
p_{0}
,
p_{1}
of degrees
\leq 2n
such that (see (
49
))

(z-{\overline{z}})^{n+1}f(z)=d_{n}\Big[\phi(z)+\sum_{s=0,1}p_{s}(z)\log(z-s)\Big]+{\overline{d}}_{n}\Big[\overline{\phi}({\overline{z}})+(-1)^{n}\sum_{s=0,1}p_{s}({\overline{z}})\log({\overline{z}}-{\overline{s}})\Big].

We consider the
\log(z{\overline{z}})
term in the expansion (
18
) of
f(z)
at
z=0
. The only contribution comes from

[d_{n}p_{0}(z)]\log(z)+(-1)^{n}[{\overline{d}}_{n}p_{0}({\overline{z}})]\log({\overline{z}})
. By (
141
) this equals
[d_{n}p_{0}(z)]\log(z{\overline{z}})
.
By Lemma
96
there exists a symmetric polynomial
P
of degrees
\leq n
in
z
and
{\overline{z}}
such that

\text{$\log(z{\overline{z}})$ coefficient of $f(z)$ }=\frac{P(z,{\overline{z}})}{(z-{\overline{z}})^{2n+1}}.

In the following we consider
z
and
{\overline{z}}
as independent variables. If
P(z,{\overline{z}})\neq 0
, the coefficient has singularity at
z={\overline{z}}
. This is ruled out by (G3).
(Because the right hand side is anti-symmetric,
P(z,{\overline{z}})=0
also follows from (G1).)
Therefore
d_{n}p_{0}(z)=0
and because
d_{n}
is injective in the space of analytic functions (this is the complex conjugate of the last statement in the proof
of Theorem
34
) we have
p_{0}(z)=0
. Likewise we get
p_{1}(z)=0
.

Assume
\phi(z)
has a pole of order
m>0
at
z=0
. Then
d_{n}\phi(z)
has a pole of order
n+m
at
z=0
(from the summand
k=0
in
d_{n}
).
The term
{\overline{d}}_{n}\overline{\phi}({\overline{z}})
is analytic at
z=0
(it may have poles at
{\overline{z}}=0
). Therefore
(z-{\overline{z}})^{n+1}f(z)
(and hence also
f(z)
) has a pole of order
n+m
at
z=0
.
On the other hand the graph
G_{1}
has a single edge of weight 1 connected to
z
so that
\nu_{z}^{>}=1
and
\nu_{0z}=0
in (G3).
We get
M_{0}\geq-n
which rules out poles of order
>n
in
f(z)
. Therefore
\phi(z)
is analytic at
z=0
. Likewise
\phi(z)
is analytic at
z=1
which makes
\phi(z)
a polynomial in
z
.

Let
m
be the degree of the polynomial
\phi(z)
and assume
m>2n
. We consider the expansion of
f(z)
at
z=\infty
.
From
d_{n}\phi(z)
we get a leading order
z^{m-n}
with coefficient

\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{k}\frac{(n+k)!m!}{(n-k)!k!(m-n+k)!}=\frac{(m-n-1)!}{(m-2n-1)!},

where we used the second identity in (
131
) for
a=m-2n
,
b=c=n
. The term
{\overline{d}}_{n}\overline{\phi}({\overline{z}})
is of order
\leq n<m-n
for
z\to\infty
.
We conclude that in the expansion of
f(z)
at
z=\infty
we have a term of order
z^{m-2n-1}
.
On the other hand we have
\nu_{z}^{<}=0
and
\nu_{z}=1
which implies
M_{\infty}\leq-1
in (G3). This rules out the case
m>2n
. So,
\phi(z)
is a polynomial of degree
\leq 2n
in
z
.
Likewise
\overline{\phi}({\overline{z}})
is a polynomial of degree
\leq 2n
in
{\overline{z}}
. Like for the coefficient of
\log(z{\overline{z}})
there exists a symmetric polynomial
P\in{\mathbb{C}}[z,{\overline{z}}]

of degrees
\leq n
in
z
and
{\overline{z}}
such that
f(z)=P(z,{\overline{z}})/(z-{\overline{z}})^{2n+1}
. By (G3) (or by (G1)) we get
P(z,{\overline{z}})=0
.

25.
The algorithm for appending an edge of weight 1

Let
n=D/2-2
. By repeated single-valued integration we calculate

(147)

g(z)=(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}[\mathcal{I}_{n}+D_{n}(1-\Delta_{n}\mathcal{I}_{n})](z-{\overline{z}})^{n+1}f_{G}(z)\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}

(if possible). By Lemma
30
and Theorem
34
we get

(148)

-n!(z-{\overline{z}})^{2n+1}f_{G_{1}}(z)=g(z)-h(z),

with

(149)

h(z)=(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}d_{n}\Big[\phi(z)+\sum_{s=0,1}p_{s}(z)\log(z-s)\Big]+(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}{\overline{d}}_{n}\Big[\overline{\phi}({\overline{z}})+(-1)^{n}\sum_{s=0,1}p_{s}({\overline{z}})\log({\overline{z}}-{\overline{s}})\Big],

where
\phi\in{\mathbb{C}}[z,z^{-1},(z-1)^{-1}]
,
\overline{\phi}\in{\mathbb{C}}[{\overline{z}},{\overline{z}}^{-1},({\overline{z}}-1)^{-1}]
, and
p_{0}
,
p_{1}
, are polynomials of degrees
\leq 2n
.
By partial fraction decomposition we write

\displaystyle\phi(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0,1}\phi_{s}(z)+\phi_{\text{reg}}(z)+\phi_{\infty}(z),\quad\text{where}

(150)

\displaystyle\phi_{s}(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=K_{s}}^{-1}c_{k}^{s}(z-s)^{k},\quad\phi_{\text{reg}}(z)=\sum_{k=0}^{2n}c_{k}^{\infty}z^{k},\quad\phi_{\infty}(z)=\sum_{k=2n+1}^{K_{\infty}}c_{k}^{\infty}z^{k},

with an analogous decomposition for
\overline{\phi}({\overline{z}})
.

25.1.
Solving for
\phi_{s}(z)
and
\overline{\phi}_{s}({\overline{z}})

For
s=0,1
we expand
g(z)
at
s
in
z
and
{\overline{z}}
up to order
n
yielding the truncated single-valued log-Laurent series
g_{s}(z)
.

By (G3) the graphical function
f_{G_{1}}(z)
admits an expansion at
z=s
with terms
\log^{\ell}(|z-s|^{2})(z-s)^{m}({\overline{z}}-{\overline{s}})^{\overline{m}}
with
m,{\overline{m}}\geq-n
.
By (
148
) the total degree (i.e.
m+{\overline{m}}
) of any term in the expansion of
g(z)-h(z)
at
z=s
is thus
\geq 1
.
Because the differential operators
(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}d_{n}
and
(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}{\overline{d}}_{n}
do not alter the total degree, terms of negative total degree in
h(z)
at
z=s

can only come from
\phi_{s}(z)
and
\overline{\phi}_{s}({\overline{z}})
. Therefore

(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}[d_{n}\phi_{s}(z)+{\overline{d}}_{n}\overline{\phi}_{s}({\overline{z}})]=g_{s}(z)\quad\text{for negative total degree.}

Note that the left hand side has maximal degree
n
in
z
and
{\overline{z}}
(from the term
k=0
in the sums of
d_{n}
and
{\overline{d}}_{n}
).
Therefore all pole-terms are contained in
g_{s}(z)
. We read off
(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}d_{n}\phi_{s}(z)
from the negative powers of
z
in
g_{s}(z)
.
The coefficient of
{\overline{z}}^{n}
in
(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}d_{n}\phi_{s}(z)
is
(-1)^{n}\partial_{z}^{n}\phi_{s}(z)
. This gives
\partial_{z}^{n}\phi_{s}(z)
which can trivially be integrated
n
times. Integration constants
are insignificant because they do not affect the pole terms in
\phi_{s}(z)
.

The procedure to obtain
\overline{\phi}_{s}({\overline{z}})
is analogous starting from negative powers of
{\overline{z}}
in
g_{s}(z)
.

25.2.
Solving for
\phi_{\infty}(z)
and
\overline{\phi}_{\infty}({\overline{z}})

We expand
g(z)
at
\infty
to order
-n-1
in
z^{-1}
and
-n
in
{\overline{z}}^{-1}
yielding the truncated single-valued log-Laurent series
g_{\infty}(z)
.

By (G3) the graphical function
f_{G_{1}}(z)
admits an expansion at
z=\infty
with terms
\log^{\ell}(|z|^{2})z^{m}{\overline{z}}^{\overline{m}}
with
m,{\overline{m}}\leq-1
.
The total degree of any term in the expansion of
g(z)-h(z)
at
z=\infty
is thus
\geq-2n+1
. Terms of total degree
\leq-2n-1
in
h(z)
at
z=\infty

come from
\phi_{\infty}(z)
and
\overline{\phi}_{\infty}({\overline{z}})
. Therefore

(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}[d_{n}\phi_{\infty}(z)+{\overline{d}}_{n}\overline{\phi}_{\infty}({\overline{z}})]=g_{\infty}(z)\quad\text{for total degree}\leq-2n-1\text{ in }z^{-1}.

In
(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}d_{n}\phi_{\infty}(z)
the coefficient of
{\overline{z}}^{n}
is
(-1)^{n}\partial_{z}^{n}\phi_{\infty}(z)
whereas in
(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}{\overline{d}}_{n}\overline{\phi}_{\infty}({\overline{z}})
all terms have degree

\leq-n-1
in
{\overline{z}}^{-1}
. From the
{\overline{z}}^{n}
coefficient of
g_{\infty}(z)
we read off
\partial_{z}^{n}\phi_{\infty}(z)
which can trivially be integrated to
obtain
\phi_{\infty}(z)
(integration constants are insignificant).

To obtain
\overline{\phi}_{\infty}({\overline{z}})
we expand
g(z)
at
\infty
to order
-n
in
z^{-1}
and
-n-1
in
{\overline{z}}^{-1}
. The analog of the above algorithm gives

\overline{\phi}_{\infty}({\overline{z}})
. Because expansions at infinity can be time consuming, it is faster to compute the orders
-n-1,-n
and
-n,-n-1
than a single expansion
to order
-n,-n
in
z^{-1},{\overline{z}}^{-1}
.

25.3.
Solving for
p_{s}(z)

Terms with
\log(|z-s|^{2})
in
h(z)
come from (see (
141
))

(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}[d_{n}p_{s}(z)\log(z-s)+(-1)^{n}{\overline{d}}_{n}p_{s}({\overline{z}})\log({\overline{z}}-{\overline{s}})]=(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}\log(|z-s|^{2})d_{n}p_{s}(z)+\text{non-log terms.}

The function
g\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{V}_{\{0,1,\infty\}}
has a single-valued log-Laurent expansion at
z=s
of the form (
18
),

g(z)=\sum_{\ell}\sum_{m,{\overline{m}}}g_{\ell,m,{\overline{m}}}^{s}\log^{\ell}(|z-s|^{2})(z-s)^{m}({\overline{z}}-{\overline{s}})^{\overline{m}}.

We set
z={\overline{z}}
in the
\log(|z-s|^{2})
-coefficient of this expansion. Because of the factor
(z-{\overline{z}})^{2n+1}
on the left hand side of (
148
) we have
h(z)=g(z)
in this limit.
In
(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}d_{n}
only the summand
k=n
survives. We get

(151)

(-1)^{n}\frac{(2n)!}{n!}p_{s}(z)=\sum_{m,{\overline{m}}}g_{1,m,{\overline{m}}}^{s}(z-s)^{m+{\overline{m}}}.

On the other hand we get from (G1) and (
148
) that
g(z)-h(z)
is anti-symmetric under
z\leftrightarrow{\overline{z}}
.
From Lemma
96
we know that
(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}d_{n}p_{s}(z)
is a symmetric polynomial of degree
\leq n
in
z
and
{\overline{z}}
.
Therefore, the expansion coefficients
g_{1,m,{\overline{m}}}^{s}
with
m
or
{\overline{m}}
not in
\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
are anti-symmetric,

g_{1,m,{\overline{m}}}^{s}=-g_{1,{\overline{m}},m}^{s}\quad\text{if not }m,{\overline{m}}\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}.

This anti-symmetry lets the sum over
m
and
{\overline{m}}
on the right hand side of (
151
) collapse to values in
\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
,

p_{s}(z)=(-1)^{n}\frac{n!}{(2n)!}\sum_{m,{\overline{m}}=0}^{n}g_{1,m,{\overline{m}}}^{s}(z-s)^{m+{\overline{m}}}.

With this restriction the coefficients
g_{1,m,{\overline{m}}}^{s}
are in
g_{s}(z)
.

25.4.
Solving for
\phi_{\text{reg}}(z)
and
\overline{\phi}_{\text{reg}}({\overline{z}})

We expand

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}(z)=g(z)

\displaystyle-(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}d_{n}\Big[\phi_{\infty}(z)+\sum_{s=0,1}\phi_{s}(z)+p_{s}(z)\log(z-s)\Big]

\displaystyle-\,(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}{\overline{d}}_{n}\Big[\overline{\phi}_{\infty}({\overline{z}})+\sum_{s=0,1}\overline{\phi}_{s}({\overline{z}})+(-1)^{n}p_{s}({\overline{z}})\log({\overline{z}}-{\overline{s}})\Big]

in
z=0
up to order
n
yielding

\widetilde{g}_{\text{reg}}(z)=\sum_{\ell}\sum_{m,{\overline{m}}\leq n}\widetilde{g}^{\text{reg}}_{\ell,m,{\overline{m}}}\log^{\ell}(z{\overline{z}})z^{m}{\overline{z}}^{\overline{m}}.

By (
141
) we have

(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}{\overline{d}}_{n}\overline{\phi}_{\text{reg}}({\overline{z}})=(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}d_{n}\overline{\phi}_{\text{reg}}(z).

So, the contributions of
\phi_{\text{reg}}(z)
and
\overline{\phi}_{\text{reg}}({\overline{z}})
to
h(z)
can be combined to
(z-{\overline{z}})^{n}d_{n}[\phi_{\text{reg}}(z)+\overline{\phi}_{\text{reg}}(z)]
.
We can hence set

\overline{\phi}_{\mathrm{reg}}({\overline{z}})=0

without restriction. We follow the construction of
p_{s}(z)
in the previous subsection, now using the logarithm-free part of the expansion of
\widetilde{g}_{\text{reg}}(z)
.
In complete analogy we get

\phi_{\text{reg}}(z)=(-1)^{n}\frac{n!}{(2n)!}\sum_{m,{\overline{m}}=0}^{n}\widetilde{g}^{\text{reg}}_{0,m,{\overline{m}}}z^{m+{\overline{m}}}.
